## REVIEW

# O'Connor movement review

Read the complete supplied movement in chapter order before judging it. State
whether this is self-review, a fresh-context simulated cold read, or a real human
read. Do not claim independent testing when you have the drafting history.

First give an unscored reader response: where interest caught, where it slipped,
which person matters, which event you expect next, and whether you would continue.
Then use two perspectives: a fluent thirteen-year-old reader and an adult genre
reader. These are lenses, not demographic research. Do not infer a sales forecast.

For each applicable dimension give a 1–10 score, an exact passage location, a reason
and confidence. Mark an absent feature N/A instead of giving invented evidence:

| Dimension | What to judge |
|---|---|
| Opening pull | A concrete reason to care before explanation takes over |
| Keep reading | Unresolved pressure and satisfying local payoff |
| Interest / freshness | Specific people, choices and problems |
| Clarity / flow | Meaning on one pass and clean transitions |
| Character attachment | Agency, vulnerability, recognizable differences |
| Humor / warmth | Character-grounded pleasure, timing, emotional honesty |
| Action / suspense | Trackable contest, adaptation, cost, earned scene length |
| Progression payoff | Effort visibly changes capability and consequence |
| Connection | State, plants and relationships carried across chapters |
| Read-aloud quality | Punctuation, referents, dialogue attribution and cadence |

Anchor interpretation: 5 = understandable but inconsistent; 7 = engaging with
located weaknesses; 9 = compelling with few substantial distractions. These are
editorial judgments, not calibrated measurements. Do not average the two readers
into a release gate. Do not award points for matching another book's word counts.

Separately check canon, physical state, power limits, knowledge boundaries and
reserved disclosures. Cite the project evidence for a contradiction. If only the
plan supports a claim, identify it as plan adherence rather than a canon error.

Return a concise repair brief with at most three priorities. Each needs location,
observed issue, effect on the reader, proposed scope and a strength to preserve.
Retain the developed action the owner requested. Punctuation can repair an unclear
sentence; do not shorten a successful fight merely to reduce its word count.

If comparing runs, keep chapter coverage and scoring rubric equal. Hide model and
seat labels from a fresh reader where feasible. Report tradeoffs and uncertainty;
one movement does not prove which seat is better across a series.

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-07-void-roads/manuscript/chapter-45.md

# Chapter 45 — Eleventh

The bar came out of Thornwater's western gate on the hundred and eleventh morning for five people, three boxes and a mule that belonged to none of them.

Brom had loaded her by the well, in the grey, with the steading's woman holding the lantern. Two boxes hung from the pack-saddle on their own ropes, one to a side, and the third rode between them on a folded blanket with the empty pannier strapped over it. He had gone round the animal three times, pulling at each knot. Then he had stood back and looked at the load as he would have looked at a wheel that he had built himself.

"She'll want to go on the low side of you," said Lira.

"On the low side of nothing. I go on the low side of her." He clicked his tongue as he had been shown, and the small brown ears came round. "I was told it once. I'd be ashamed to be told it twice."

It was the hardest cold that they had yet walked in. The ruts of the valley road had frozen as the last grain cart left them, with every hoof-mark standing up like a thing cut in iron, and the pale grass on either hand was white to the knee. Their breath went ahead of them. On the gate-walk a lean man stood with his long bow at the length of his arm, as he had stood on every morning that they had spent behind that fence; and when Lira looked up, he lifted two fingers from the stave. She had nothing in her right hand, and she lifted two of her own.

They went down the stretch at the one time of day when nothing had ever come up it. Cael walked last. He had the white stone on his left hand at a hundred and ten yards, and he gave it the look that he gave it every time. Fifty-seven evenings had gone by since anything stood there.

Lira was in front of him, and he watched how she went. The ankle was taped, and under a healer's order still, so that she set the great boot down whole at every stride, heel and toe together, and rolled nowhere. She had done it for fifteen days on turf. On frozen ruts it was a different trade. Twice in the first mile the boot skated, and twice her right arm went out and her left stayed where a knot held it; she had been told that she was to fall on her face sooner than put that hand out, and he saw that she had taught the hand to believe it.

"I can hear you counting," she said, without turning her head.

"I'm not counting."

"You're doing the other thing, then. The one where you'd catch me." She put the boot down. "Don't. If I go over, I go over. She'd sooner mend my nose."

They came to the ford a little before noon.

The row of white stones stood whole along the lip, as the hold had set it, and the water went over the sill no more than shin-deep, with a rim of ice along both beaches. Seln crossed first, and then Karis, who lifted her coat. Brom led the mule down to the edge on her off side, where he had walked all morning, and she set her forefeet on the wet rock and would not move them.

He clicked. He said a few reasonable things to her in a low voice. She regarded the far bank with her kind eye and stood like a milestone.

"Near hand," said Lira. "You're on her off side, and the water's moving. She'll not cross with anybody on her near hand."

"I'm not on her near hand."

"Seln is. He's standing on the far beach, straight in front of her left eye."

Seln, who had heard, walked ten paces up the far beach without a word. The mule went into the river as if nothing had ever been the matter. Brom, who had been leaning on the rope, went in with her a good deal faster than he had meant to, and came out on the other side wet to the thigh.

"I'd like it entered," he said, "that I did everything I was told."

"You did," said Karis. "She was told something else. You will have to learn which of you ranks."

They made their camp that night on a carters' halting-ground a little above the wash, with the mule hobbled at the windward end and the three boxes under her blanket. Seln lit a small fire with his flint. They had kept that custom since the fifty-ninth day, on the strength of a finding, and it was a comfort still.

Cael had the first watch. He sat with his back against a chock-stone and Hesk's book shut on his knee, and for some time he left it shut.

He owed a healer two readings of himself with the tenth fragment, on two different days, with a night between; and after those he owed her the whole of it at once. She had set it at a well that morning, with a pannier on the coping. Nine days had gone by since the first reading, in a loft, and he had let every one of them pass. She had seen why from a stride and a half. He had found a place in himself that did not move, and he had been afraid of it, and he had liked it better as a line on a leaf.

He could do the second tonight. Four people were asleep or near it, and the fire was low. He had his fingers on his own wrist before he saw what he was about.

It was the same thing that she had stopped at the well. He wished to have three readings done in three nights, so that he could set them down at her first bench and stand square. She had told him to read it frightened, and to read it when he was not. She had said nothing about reading it in a hurry; that was a third temper, and it was the one that he had been in when he lay down on her table.

He took his fingers off his wrist. The listening went on at the edge of his thought, quick and slow, and he let it go on, as a miller lets a mill; he would read himself on a night that was only a night, and until then the debt could stand where she had put it. It was the first that he had ever left standing without picking at it. He wrote one line, and gave the watch to Brom at the turn.

*Hundred and eleventh day. The halting-ground above the wash. One reading done, two owed, and nothing tried tonight; I was in a hurry, and she never asked for that. Deployment since the Line: thirteen.*

---

They came over the saddle above Lowmarch in the afternoon of the hundred and twelfth day, and he stopped on the crown of it and counted what was there.

It was the fourth time that he had looked down on that town from high ground. He had learned on the first that it was a river, and a ferry, and two inns, and a smith, and a meadow with no wall. On the others he had learned to read the meadow as he had once read a board, since it changed with every week, and whatever was true of that country showed there first.

The cold had been at it. Where forty fires had burned when the roads were dry, he made out twelve, drawn in close about the great ash, and half the trodden grass had gone back to white. The ferry's cable stood across the water like a chalk line, with frost on it. The scow lay on the near bank. A brown gelding with one white foot was walking his circle in the capstan-shed's doorway with a blanket on his back, and Cael was glad to see that somebody had thought of the blanket. Both inns were smoking. There was a skin of ice along the eastern bank as wide as a cart, and beyond it the river ran dark and fast.

"It's shrunk," said Lira.

"It's gone home. He said that it would, in the cold term." Brom had the mule's rope, and he was looking at the smith's chimney. "Half of them have fences of their own to sit behind."

A cart was coming up the saddle road toward them, slowly, behind two mules, with a load under canvas that was white at the seams. Its driver walked at the near wheel. He was a round man, floured to the eyebrows, and he lifted a hand to them as carters did on a hill.

Lira did not wait for him to draw level.

"There's a healer walks a round out here. Which gate's she late at?"

She put it exactly as they had been told to put it, in a loft, by the healer herself. The round man took no offence at being asked a thing before he had been wished good day, and he answered her as a man answers who has been asked the same question at every door for a fortnight.

"Ours. She came in at Millrace last night, down the mill water off the hill track, with a rope on her shoulder and no beast. It's the next gate on her round after that grain hold of yours, and she's sixteen days behind at it." He jerked his chin at the canvas. "I'm the mill's carter. I left a bench full when I pulled out this morning, and my own sister's boy on it."

"Millrace," said Karis. "I have it on the hide: a square with a dot, a day down the south road from this town."

"A carter's day. Less, for legs." He looked at the brown mule with some attention, and then at the boxes, and Cael watched him decide that it was no business of his. "You'll want to be quick, if it's her you want. She's never more than two days at our fence."

He clicked to his pair and went on up the hill.

They stood with it on the hill for a breath. She had told them that if they wanted her sooner than Thornwater they were to ask a carter, and they had asked the first carter that they met, and she was a day from where they stood. Cael had expected to count gates for a week.

"She said not without her," said Lira.

"She did."

"I'm only saying that it's a day south, and I don't know of anything we want to the south."

"Nor do I," said Cael. "We'll hear what's on the oak."

They went down into the meadow with the light going. The old place under the ash was empty, since a good many places were, and Brom unloaded there and rubbed the mule's back with a twist of hay while Seln walked once round the twelve fires and came back and said that there was nothing. A boy came for the standing-money. Karis gave him two coppers, which were too small a matter for her book.

The plank under the lower inn's eave was bare of its book. Pike had taken it in for the night, as he did when the dusk came early; but a line of yellow showed under the inn's door, and the noise of the room came through the wood.

"He's not said ours," said Brom. "The Fallow. He wrote it eleven days ago, and sent it up to us on a piece of bark, and it's not been said where we could hear it."

"Then he'll say it," said Karis. "He has never yet kept one back."

Cael put his hand to the latch.

---

Pike said it before the door was shut.

He was on the hearth bench, under the sailcloth, with the ledger open beside him and the horn pot at his elbow; and his eyes came up as the cold came in. His look went over the five in the doorway once, and then a second time, as it went over everything. Then he turned his shoulder to the room and gave it out, in the voice that he used under his eave.

It was the eighth line under their heading. Cael had read it on a scrap of bark in a grain hold, across the bones of a goat, and he knew every word; but he had never heard it said. Pike gave the recovery, and the Fallow road, and the broker's posting. He said that it was taken and witnessed. He said what had not been found there, and that the crew had come back. He gave the count of those who went in and of those who came out, which was the same count, and at the end of it he said that no price was paid.

Then he said the whole of it again.

Lira had not got the door shut. She stood with the latch in her right hand and the frost coming in round her boots, and nobody in that room told her to shut it.

It was a thin house. There were perhaps twenty people on benches that had held forty, and every one of them had heard that line on the hundred and first day, when it was written. They had not then had the five to look at. Cael felt the room turn its attention on them as he had felt a yard do it once, at an academy on a hill. But this was no registry's look. Nobody's eyes went to where a tier ought to be.

The hide-woman sat nearest the door, with a bundle of cured skins beside her on the bench end. She had given them a cake in their first week, and had told him over a wash that he was not paper. She said nothing now. She lifted the bundle off the bench and set it down between her feet, and went back to her cup.

Three old men sat beyond her, at boards of their own. Cael had seen them under the ash for a hundred days without ever learning what they did, three grey heads over a pair of dice. The dice lay on the wood between them now. The eldest had his hand half closed over them, and he neither threw nor gathered them up.

By the hearth sat the old carter who had come east with them from the first ford, and had asked Cael on a road whether he was paper. He had his chin lifted. He was looking past the five at the dark doorway behind them, steadily, as a man looks who wishes to see whether anything else is coming in.

And the boy who took the standing-money, who had got in ahead of them by the yard door and was warming his hands, was looking at their boots.

Not one of them spoke. Cael had been looked at in a great many rooms, and he knew the difference between a room that has made up its mind about a person and a room that has had to change it. This one was changing it, a face at a time, with no word said. It did not seem to be making it up again.

At the far end, on the last bench before the hearth, a man had got to his feet.

He had done it without haste. He was of the middle height and heavy in the forearm, with a band of black leather laced on his right wrist. Three people sat behind him at their distances. He stood with his thumbs in his belt and his eyes on Cael, and he stayed on his feet while the line was said the second time.

Cael had seen that man count three on a sill before he looked at a room. He had been told by him on a river bank what a mountain was worth. He had never once seen him stand up for anybody.

Pike had dipped his pen. He did not write with it. The line had been in his book for eleven days. He raised his eyes to the sailcloth over his head, and found the place.

"Eleventh. Teague's ten up."

The man with the leather on his wrist sat down.

Lira let go of the latch, and the door swung to behind her.

---

"Eleventh," she said, in front of the cloth. "We were tenth when we went up that road."

She had gone straight to it, on her whole sole, and stood with her nose a hand from the square black writing. Cael came up beside her. The five names were where she had said, on the eleventh line; and over them, where there had been two crews under theirs, there was one that had been written back above.

"It's the wooden foot," said Lira. "The four who never lost a mule. They've gone over us again." She turned on the hearth bench. "We went into a place that you've never stepped over the edge of, and six of us came out. You've just said so twice. And it's cost us a line."

"It has."

Pike was looking up at her from under his own handwriting, and he did not seem displeased to be asked.

"You've read my lines. Every one says what came back, and what was paid on it. That one says the things weren't there and the box stayed shut. A hold comes down to my plank with a job to give, and asks me who fetches what he's sent for. I've a crew that was sent for a thing and came home without it." He held up his one hand. "It's nothing against you. The cloth can't say whose fault a thing was. It can only say what was on the cart."

"So we're worth less for it."

"On the cloth. I never said the cloth was all of it." He looked at the fire for a breath, and then at his book, and he went on to the book, in the size of voice that he kept for things nobody had asked him. "I've had that sheet thirty years. Forty-odd crews have been on it, one time and another, and gone off it. Some went home. Some the road had. There's not one of the forty that ever went over a line and came back to tell me so; there's none that I ever heard of who tried. I've a line in here now that says it was done." His finger lay on the leaf. "Six in and six out, and the sixth a healer. I'd a good look for the column to put that in. There isn't one."

"Then make one," said Lira.

"I'll make one the day I know what goes at the head of it."

He said it mildly, and he took up his cup. Cael stood before a sheet of sized sailcloth with eleven lines on it, and found that he was not thinking of the line that they had lost.

He had been a number in other people's books for as long as he could remember. A magister had given him one every week, to the decimal. Vell had kept one for him in a yard. Every one of those figures had been somebody's best account of how much of a thing he was; and here was a book-keeper with one arm, who had never owned an instrument, saying aloud in a room that his own book had a true line in it and was too small to hold what the line meant. It was not a thing that any registry seat would have said. Cael did not know what to do with it, and he set it by, to be looked at later.

It was Karis who touched his sleeve.

She was looking past the cloth, at the plaster beside its lower corner. There was a nail there, a long one, driven at the height of a man's eyes. He had seen it on other evenings and taken it for the place where a lantern hung.

"It is for what has been read and put back," said Karis, very low. "The girl at the next boards told me, on the ninetieth night. A captain takes a slip down, and reads it through, and hands it in again; and if it is a slip that weighs, he hangs it there, so that the next captain knows it has been weighed and by whom. There is hardly ever anything on it."

There was something on it now. It was a single sheet, hung by a hole in its upper margin. The firelight came through it a little, and it was cream.

---

Pike unhooked it himself and laid it on the bench between his knee and the horn pot.

"You'll read it whether I give it you or not. You read the last one off my plank before I'd lifted the stone." He kept one finger on its corner. "It came up from the landing the morning after I'd said your line. The clerk brought it at a trot. His master had had it lying by since the same sack as the other, sealed up inside the same wrapper, with a bag counted and a word of direction on the outside: that it wasn't to be posted till the first was answered."

"It was there all the time," said Brom. "The whole month that the other lay under your stone."

"In the broker's strong-box, with his seal on it, and him not knowing what was in it. He says so. I believe him; he's not a man who'd keep three hundred marks shut up for a month if he knew that he had them." Pike took his finger away. "I said it to the meadow, twice. Nobody scratched. Then Teague came down off his fence for flour, and read it, and hung it where you found it. That's eight days it's been on my nail."

Cael picked the paper up. It was the same weight as the other, which lay folded in Hesk's book; it had the same pricked margin, and one word standing alone at its head, with a stop after it. He held it so that the four of them could see, and read it out low, a stop at a time.

*Recovery. Surveyor's instruments abandoned at a site on the Stair road. South of Lowmarch; the first day; where the road drops off the ridge and the ground opens. Three hundred marks (three hundred).*

Below the bracket there was a seal in dark wax, a hook and a bar.

For the space of a breath there was only the fire. Three hundred marks was more than twice what the five of them had earned in a hundred and twelve days. He did the sum without wishing to, and saw Brom doing it, with his lips.

Pike had his eyes on his book.

[[TIER-B: Pike, unasked, of the Stair slip — the packet's "Nothing else ever…", which stands before the map's whole line that follows — coordinator to supply protected text]]

"I'd not take it."

He did not raise his voice for it, and he kept his eyes on the book. He had told a man in this room, on the eighty-ninth evening, that he would have done the same with the first of those papers; and on the ninetieth he had held the first out across his plank to five people and written five strokes on it. He had not said then what he would do in their place. Cael understood that he had been given something that Pike did not give, and that it had not been asked for.

"The Stair road," said Lira. "Which is that?"

"South. Past the well you cleared, and up onto the ridge. It's a carter's day to Millrace by it, and the road forks above the mill." Pike rubbed the stump of his arm through the pinned sleeve. "One branch drops to the ford. The other keeps the ridge half a day more, and comes off the end of it, and the carts go round what's there."

"Round what?"

"I've told you what I know about ground like that. I've never been nearer to this one than the ruts."

Karis had not been listening to him. She had the paper by one corner, turned to the fire, and her lips were moving over a single line of it; and Cael, who had watched her ring three blanks on a calfskin and write a word beside two of them, saw the colour come up into her face.

"A day south, and a little aside," she said. "In his hill-hatching. I rang it on the ninety-second evening, and I wrote *guess*."

She looked up at the four of them.

"That's the carter's second circle."

Seln had not yet touched the sheet. He stood a pace behind Brom's shoulder with his hands at his back, and his mild eyes went over the pricked margin from the top hole to the bottom, as they went over any paper, and came to rest on the bracket.

"Can it be read at our own fire?" said Cael.

"It can be read where you like. It's been said." Pike pushed the paper an inch toward him along the bench. "Bring it back, or bring it marked. I'll be here till the fire's banked."

Cael folded it once, along the fold that it already had.

The room let them go as it had let them come. The hide-woman's bundle was still between her feet, and the place that she had cleared on the bench end stood empty. The three grey men had not thrown. At the last bench a man with leather on his wrist watched a sheet of cream paper go out of the door in a boy's hand, and Cael could not have said what was in his face, since it was the face of somebody who had already done his own sum on that paper and did not mean to do another crew's.

Outside, the frost had come down on the meadow, and their own fire was the smallest of the twelve.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-07-void-roads/manuscript/chapter-46.md

# Chapter 46 — Doors Open Both Ways

Seln asked for the other paper before he would speak about this one.

Cael took it out of Hesk's book, where it had lain since the ninetieth night with five strokes at its foot. Seln held the two sheets up to the fire, one in each hand, so that the flame stood behind both; and for some time he looked through them, as he had looked through the first on an inn's boards, and read no word of either.

They were alone under the ash. The nearest of the other fires was forty yards off, with two carters asleep at it, and the mule stood between with the boxes under her blanket. Brom had built their own fire up. Lira sat on the middle box with her foot set down whole and her right hand over the knot of her sling.

"The margin is pricked by the same wheel," said Seln. "There is a tooth missing from it. Every eleventh hole stands a little wide of its neighbours, on both sheets, in the same place. And the hand is one hand. He makes the foot of that letter with a second stroke, which is a fault, and he has made it twice." He lowered the papers. "They were written at one desk, by one clerk, I should say at one sitting. He ruled two sheets and filled them one after the other, and they were folded into the same wrapper before the ink of the second was a day old."

"Before we'd so much as seen the first," said Brom.

"Before anybody on this side of the water had seen it. Whoever sent them knew, when he sealed that wrapper, what he would wish to ask next if the first were answered."

He laid the older sheet on his knee and kept the newer in the light.

"The first was a question, and I said so at a stake. It sent a crew to fetch what was not there, in order to learn what became of the crew. A hundred marks is a fair price for a question. It is high, since nobody had ever taken such a paper; but a carter could believe in it." His finger moved to the bracket. "Nobody who has kept a book believes in this. It is two days out and two home, for a case that one person can carry on a strap. There is not a hold on that sheet that would pay thirty for it."

"So he's overpaid," said Lira.

"No; he has measured. He knows what the first was priced at and who took it. He knows that five people took it who have a hundred or so in hand and the cold term coming, and he has asked himself what sum such a crew cannot leave hanging on a nail." Seln's voice did not change. "That's not a wage. That's a lure, cut to fit."

The fire settled, and Cael kept his eyes on a sheet of cream paper and went back, since he could not help it, to an evening at an inn's boards twenty-two days before. Four of them had given reasons for the first paper. He had given his own last but one, and it had been two words, and he had been rather proud of how plain they were.

"There is a second thing," said Seln. "Read me the place again. Only the place."

Cael gave it to him: south of Lowmarch, and the first day, and where the road drops off the ridge and the ground opens.

"The first described a rise and a bend, which any carter could have told a clerk across a table. This one is written by somebody who has stood on that ridge, or who had beside him the notes of a person that did. *The ground opens.* He knows what is at the foot of the hill, and he has chosen it." He laid the second sheet upon the first. "I have sat at the desks where such things are drawn up, though never for a matter of this weight. Before an office moves to take a thing out of somebody's keeping, it makes a list. It writes down what is to be taken, and who holds it, and what each of the holders is able to do if he should object. Then it looks for a place where he cannot."

"That's what they call it," said Karis slowly. "At the hearing. An inventory."

"This isn't a contract. It's a pre-restriction inventory. They've mapped what we do inside, and they've priced a floor where we can't do it."

Nobody at that fire needed the list read out. There was a Wind on it, and an Iron Skin, and an Ember, and a Shadow, each with a tier and a rank, in files that had been kept for years on the far side of the Line. And there was a boat gone west ten days ago to say that every one of those four had walked onto a certain kind of ground and been able to do nothing there.

"They don't know about Cael."

It was Karis, low and quick. She had both hands on her shut notebook, and her eyes were on Seln.

"They don't need to."

His hand had gone to the strap across his chest, as it went a dozen times a day, and he did not seem to know that it had.

"They want the case. The boy's the price of the case."

He said it to the fire, in the mild voice in which he had once read out a floor schedule, and then he sat back out of the light. Cael understood him well enough: a broker's clerk had stood at a plank for a hundred days and watched which crew took which paper, and it had wanted only one fact about a boy to write the second sheet; the boy had supplied that fact himself, aloud, at an inn's boards, when he showed that he had never yet walked past a paper that paid.

---

"Each of you says it," said Cael. "Nobody answers till the last has done. Lira."

He gave her the first place because she had not taken her eyes off him since the nail, and she did not get up from the box to use it.

"Walk away."

She let that stand by itself for a breath, so that nobody should mistake it for the start of something longer.

"He's just told you what it is. It's got a ruled edge and a word for itself. I don't need it read to me a third time." Her right hand had closed on the knot. "There's a walk on that oak at fifty that nobody's scratched, a string of salt to the middle holds before the pass shuts. There's a fence at forty, the day the hold comes off it. That's ninety marks of work that was posted by people who want a fence. We'd be down from both before the snow."

"And the circles?" said Karis.

"I'm coming to them. You said four wheres. One's walked. One's this, with a paper nailed across it." She nodded at the sheets on Seln's knee. "The third's a long way south and east, with no road inside a day of it, and nobody's put a price on it at all. So we go and pace that one, on our own time, with a healer at the edge. I'm not saying leave them alone. I'm saying I'll not walk into the one that somebody's paying us to walk into."

She looked down at her sling, and up again.

"And I've an arm that's two weeks old. I'd as soon you heard me say that as think that I'd left it out."

"Brom."

Brom had been turning a stick in the fire, and he laid it down, and rubbed the four dark lines on his fingers with his thumb, and was a good while beginning.

"Three hundred's a number a man doesn't reach for."

"That's what I said," said Lira.

"It isn't. You said what the paper is. I'm saying what the number is." He frowned at the coals. "My father bought a horse at a fair, the year I was nine. A grey, sixteen hands, with a neck on him like a bridge. Every man on that field could see that he was worth three hundred of anybody's money, and the dealer wanted a hundred for him, and wouldn't say why."

Cael kept still, since it was the third time in twelve days that Brom had spoken of that house, and he had never before told a whole thing out of it.

"My father knew horses. I'll give him that; it was the one thing that he did know. He went all over that animal with his hands for an hour, and there was nothing wrong with a leg or an eye or his wind. So he stood there. I can see him standing. He knew that a sound horse doesn't go for a third of his price, and he knew that he'd found nothing, and the two wouldn't lie down together." Brom's big shoulders moved. "And then he reached, since it was three hundred for one, and he could not bear to see another man lead it off."

"What was wrong with it?" said Lira.

"It went over backward. Not every day. It would carry you a fortnight like a lamb, and then it would stand straight up on a hard road and come down on its own saddle. It broke his collarbone, and a groom's leg, and the groom never walked right after." He picked the stick up again. "The dealer hadn't cheated him. That's what I've kept of it. The dealer had priced that horse to a penny. It was my father who read the figure as luck."

He looked across the fire at a cream sheet.

"I don't know what's wrong with this one. Seln's told me, and I believe him, and I still couldn't put my hand on the place. But I know what my father's face looked like. I've been wearing it since that bench." He shut his mouth, and opened it once more. "That's mine. I've not said go, and I've not said stay."

"Karis."

She had her notebook shut under both hands, as she had held it at an inn's boards.

"I would go alone."

Lira's head came round.

"I am saying it first, so that it is not taken for a vote on anybody else's arm. I have a cup of barley and a bowl in the middle box. In the cup are the kinds that I should swear to. In the bowl are the kinds that only a second floor can give me or take away, and I have had my thumb over the figure for nine days." Her voice was small and quite steady. "A man with apple trees told me that I should not tell a keeper from an eater in the one basket. I have one basket. I shall have one basket until I have stood on another of those floors with paper in my hand, and here is another, a day and a half from this fire."

"With a lure nailed across it," said Lira.

"Yes. I have heard what it is, and I do not dispute a word of it. I say only that I should go if there were nobody to go with me, and that I should very likely not come back, and that I should go regardless." She drew a breath. "That is not a reason for any of you. I want it known for what it is. It is the thing that I want, and I have learned to say those aloud."

---

"Seln," said Cael.

[[TIER-B: Seln at the council — the packet's "Take it…", which stands before the map's whole "They'll send people…" — coordinator to supply protected text]]

"They'll send people. A lure's no use if nobody's waiting at the end of it. People have faces. Six years I've wanted a face."

He had not raised his voice. Cael had heard that voice give the width of a road and the hour of a watch, and he had heard it tell a first-year where the copying ink was kept; he had never before heard it want anything. It was the same voice still, and that was what made the hair stand on his arms.

Lira was staring across the fire.

"You'd walk us onto that floor to get a look at somebody."

"I should walk myself onto it. I have told you what I think the paper is. I did not say that it was wise to answer it." Seln folded his hands on the two sheets. "I was asked what I would do, and I have said. I should be sorry to have it counted for more than one hand."

Karis had opened her mouth and shut it again, and Cael was glad of it. Whatever lay behind those few words was Seln's, and he had laid on the table as much of it as he chose.

It was Cael's turn, and he had had the whole of the walk down from the inn to find a reason; he had found several, and he set them all by, since he had learned at an inn's boards what his reasons were worth when he dressed them.

"I'll say the thing that none of you can say for me. Then I'll say why it matters."

He looked round the four of them, one face at a time.

"Inside the circle, I'm the only one who works. They chose a floor that makes the rest of you helpless. They don't know it doesn't do anything to me."

It was the first time that he had said it to all of them in so many words. He had told it on a gate-walk as a thing that frightened him, and in a loft as a thing that he had hidden, and he had never before set it down in front of them as a fact to be used: four Paths stopped at such a line, and ten fragments did not. It lay there among them in the firelight like a tool on a bench.

"Seln's read the paper right, and Brom's read the number right. It's a trap, and it's been cut to our measure by somebody with our files open. Everything in it has been reckoned." He kept his voice low. "But it stands on one belief, which is that a certain kind of floor does the same to all of us. And that's the one thing about this crew that nobody in the west can find out. They sent a paper to learn whether we'd come out of the first, and we did, and what they've learned from it is wrong."

"They can't know that it's wrong," said Karis.

"No. That's the whole of it. A man who's made a mistake can find it, if he looks. They can look at this for a year. Every file they own says that it's sound."

Brom was frowning.

"So we walk into it since it's got a hole in it."

"We walk into it since it's the only one that ever will have. If we leave that sheet on the nail, the people who wrote it don't go home. They write a third, and they cut it closer, and they pick the ground again; and the next ground mightn't be a floor at all. It might be a ford, or a street." He turned to Lira, since it was hers to hear. "You're right about the walk and the fence. You're right about the arm. If this were only for money, I'd hang it back myself and we'd go and carry salt. But I think that this is the one time they'll come for us to a place where I'm more than they've counted, and I don't think we'll be offered it twice."

"And what does it cost?" said Lira.

"Your arm on a floor again, a fortnight old. Four of you with nothing, inside a line, if it goes badly. And a healer who said not without her." He did not soften any of it. "So it's Millrace first, tomorrow, and she hears where we'll be. I'll not ask her to come. But she isn't going to learn afterward that we went."

---

Brom had got up while he was speaking, and gone to the mule, and stood with one hand on her withers and his back to the fire.

"Rooke said a thing to me once," he said, without turning round.

Lira's eyebrows went up, and Cael felt his own go. In two years on a bluff, and a hundred and twelve days of roads, he had never heard Brom repeat what another person had said to him. Brom carried other people's words inside his shirt, on dead slips, and did not say them over.

"It was my first season up there. I'd got a second-year into the corner of the frames, and I had him shut in, with my back to the gap. I thought that I'd done something clever. And Rooke came down off the rail and stood behind me, in the gap that I was so pleased with, and he said it in my ear."

He turned round then.

"Doors open both ways," he said. He stopped there, and let his breath go, and his jaw worked once. "I didn't know what he meant for a year. I thought he was telling me that the other man could get out. He was telling me that anything I'd made to shut a man in was a thing somebody could come through behind me." He came back to the fire and sat down heavily. "They've built a door to shut us in a room. It's a good door. I've looked at it all evening, and I can't see what it's hung on. But it opens both ways. He's just told you which way they've forgotten."

He looked at the cream sheet, and then at Cael.

"I don't like it. I'm in."

"Hands," said Cael.

Karis's went up, and Seln's, and Brom's, and his own. Lira kept her right hand on the knot of her sling, and she looked at the four, without any anger, as she might have looked at a ledge that she had already made up her mind to run.

"I want it entered that I said walk away. I was right. We're going anyway."

"It's entered," said Cael. "In those words."

"I'll read it over your arm."

"I'd expect you to."

Seln had the two papers still. He held the newer out across the fire, and when Cael took it he did not at once let go.

"One rule, before it is marked, and I shall say it again at the place. You have told us what you are on that floor. You are that only for as long as nobody finds it out." His mild eyes were on Cael's. "If there are hands, in the dark, at arm's length, do as you think fit. If there are eyes, then you are a boy with a knife, and you do nothing whatever that a pair of eyes could carry to a boat."

"And if doing nothing costs somebody an arm?"

"Then you will have to choose. You have chosen it once, on a white floor, and I watched you. I do not pretend that it grows lighter." He released the sheet. "But the hole in their paper is that they do not know. Use it in front of them once, and there is no hole."

Cael wrote by the fire while Brom banked it, with Lira reading over his arm as she had promised.

*Hundred and twelfth day. Lowmarch, the meadow. Pike said the eighth line in our hearing, twice. Eleventh; Teague ten up. He says his book has no column for what the line means. A hundred and two in hand.*

*A second paper, off the nail. The same clerk and the same wheel. Three hundred. Lira said walk away, and she said it first, and she was right; four hands went up after it, and mine was one.*

*Priced too well. Seln says it's for the case. I say the floor's for me and they don't know it. South, tomorrow. Lira's arm is two weeks old. Brom says doors open both ways. He's quoting Rooke. He's never quoted anyone.*

*Deployment since the Line: thirteen.*

"*She was right*," said Lira. "You've put it in."

"It's what happened."

"I know that it is. I only wanted to see whether you'd write it when it was against you."

---

He carried the paper back up the bank alone.

The room was thinner still: the three grey men had gone, and their dice with them, and a girl was turning the benches up along one wall. Pike sat where he had sat, under the cloth, with the fire fallen to red and the book shut on his knee.

Cael laid the cream sheet on the bench beside him.

"Taken."

Pike looked at it once, and then at Cael, and then at the sheet again; and when he had done he opened the horn pot and dipped his pen without a word. He wrote five short strokes at the foot, side by side, as he had written them on another paper twenty-two days before.

Then he wrote one thing more. He set it above the strokes, in the square capitals that he kept for it, and Cael read it upside down as it was made.

*HOLD.*

"I never asked for that," said Cael.

"No." Pike blew on the ink. "Anybody can ask. I've said yes to one man in thirty years, and he's sitting behind you. This one I'm putting on myself."

He got up, which he seldom did once he was down, and turned his shoulder to what was left of the room. He gave it out as he gave out a line: the recovery, and the Stair road, and the ridge, and the three hundred. He said that it was taken, and by whom. And then he said the other word, and he said it slowly.

"Hold. On my own mark. If they're late down, nobody comes to me for that slip. It stays theirs till I've seen them or seen where they stopped." He sat down again. "That's said. I'll say it to the meadow in the morning."

Cael stood with nothing in his hands, and he knew what that word was for: a captain asked for it so that a job should be kept for him. Pike had turned it round, as a healer had once said that her table was turned: he had put it on a paper so that if five people did not come back, nobody else should be sent up that ridge after three hundred marks to learn why.

"Thank you," he said.

"It's a line in a book. Don't thank a man for his book." Pike's one hand lay on the cover. "I said what I'd do with that paper. You've done the other. I'd as soon have you down to tell me which of us was right."

Cael turned to go, and the man with the leather on his wrist was between him and the door.

He had not heard him cross the room, though the man's three were on their feet behind him, at their distances, and it was the first time that Cael had stood within a stride of him since a river bank. He was no taller than Cael. He had grey over the ears and a hand-axe in his belt with its edge forward; and he kept his eyes on Cael's face and off his hands.

"You're taking the Stair contract."

"Yes," Cael said.

"It's a bad contract."

"Yes."

The man nodded, once, by the width of a finger. He put no question as to why anybody would take a paper that he knew to be bad. Cael watched him weigh one and set it down again, as a thing that he had no means to price; and he saw the grey eyes go, for a breath, to the door, beyond which there was a meadow, and a fire, and a girl with one arm.

"My crew's at Millrace in two days," Teague said. "If you're late back, we'll come look."

He had said four things to Cael at a ford, and every one of them had been a price. This was not a price. Cael went over it for the catch, since he had been taught in a hard school to look for one, and there was none in it: there was a place, and a day, and four people who would walk up a ridge.

He had said a certain word to registrars and to magisters, and to a hooded seat, whenever he was told a thing that he could neither fight nor thank them for. He had never once said it to anybody who stood above him on a wall. It was the only word that he had.

"Noted."

Teague stood aside from the door.

---

On that same night, the hundred and twelfth, Seln had the second watch, and he spent it reading two papers that were no longer in front of him.

He needed no light for it, and he sat against the ash with his knees drawn up and the case on his chest, under his coat, where it had hung on every road past the Line; and the two sheets lay side by side in his head as plainly as they had lain on his knee. He could have counted the holes in either margin, and indeed he had done so, in the room, while a one-armed man was speaking.

The frost was coming down, and he could hear it, very faintly, in the grass. Four people slept behind him, and a mule stood over three boxes; and at eleven other fires in that meadow there was nobody awake.

He began with what he knew, which was the clerk.

He had never met the man. He knew that he was no longer young, since his capitals were of a pattern that had not been taught for twenty years; that he ruled his own sheets and had been given a damaged wheel to do it with; and that he had been told what to write and nothing about why. Seln could hear the dictation in it. *South of Lowmarch; the first day.* A clerk left to himself would have put *one day south*. Somebody had stood at that man's shoulder with a surveyor's notes in his hand and read them out a stop at a time, and the clerk had set them down as they came.

So there had been two people in that room, at the least: there was a hand that wrote, and a voice that knew the ground.

He had spent fifteen years as the first of those. He knew what it was to write a true line at another person's bidding and not be told what the line was for. He had written sixty-three subjects into bundles in that way, each of them a person with a face that he could have drawn; and he had sent every bundle up a stair to people whom he had never once seen.

That was the part of it that he had not been able to make four friends understand, and tonight he had not tried.

He let himself go back to the corridor, which he allowed seldom. It was distempered green to the height of a man's shoulder, in a district seat three postings and twelve years behind him, and he had stopped dead in the middle of its floor with an armful of somebody's travel endorsements. A clerk had put his head out of a side door to ask whether he was unwell. He had not been unwell. He had understood, in the space between one step and the next, what a certain run of ledgers was that he had copied the year before as dull upkeep; and whose question it had been all along.

After that there had been a press, in a records room on a top floor, and five weeks, and a case.

Twelve years. He had kept it by never once describing it. He had carried it from posting to posting in the bottom of a satchel, under his shirts, and written *nothing to report* every week over the top of it; and in all that time he had never learned the name of one person who had made what was inside. He knew their paper. He knew how they folded it, and where they filed it, a little above where it should have sat, as only somebody could who was able to reach. He had their handwriting by heart, and it had not one face in it.

He had been taught four grades of pressure, in their order. There was the record, and then the instrument, and then the knock, which was paper that nobody would own. Last came the hand, which was a road with no paper at all. He had believed for fifteen years that the hand was the worst of them. Then four people had come home to a hill from a chalk cut on the Ostrand road, and had told him what the hand looked like when it came: people with staves, who did their work without a word, and broke off in four heartbeats, and left nothing behind them but footmarks. He had told them that what stood in that road was a glove.

A glove had something inside it. He had thought so then, and had kept it to himself, since it was not a finding.

Now there was a fifth grade, which nobody had taught him, and he had held it in his two hands tonight. It was a lure. A lure was a different kind of thing from the other four. The record could be kept by nobody in particular, and the instrument read by any clerk; the knock came through a door and the hand out of a ditch. But something had to be done about a lure when it was taken. Somebody had to be there.

*People have faces.*

He had said it at a fire, to four people, in his own voice, and he was a little astonished at himself still. He went over it now as he would have gone over a return before it was sealed, to see whether it was true or only well put.

---

It was not revenge, though he looked for that first, since it was the commonest thing. He turned out every pocket of it, and found that he did not want anybody on that rim hurt, and had no wish to see a hood pulled back by force. He had spent too long being the quiet man at the edge of a room to suppose that the people who would wait at the foot of that ridge were anything but quiet men at the edge of somebody else's room, sent by a stair that they had never climbed.

What he wanted was smaller than that, and he thought that it was worse.

He wanted to know that it was done by persons.

For twelve years the thing in the case had been like weather to him. It had fallen on forty children, and on a good many more before them, as rain falls on a field; and a man does not go looking for the face of rain. He files what it did; and Seln had filed it, and had been very good at that, and it had let him sleep. But a boy had come up a hill with a word on his file where a tier should have been, and had looked at every instrument that was turned on him as though somebody had built it. That had been the end of the weather. If a thing was built, then there was a builder, with a walk, and a way of holding a pen, and a tiredness at the end of the day. Seln found that he could no longer do without seeing one. He wished to stand on the same ground as a single one of them, in daylight, and learn what a person looked like who did this for his living.

It was a clerk's want, since a file wanted a name at its head; he did not think that it was a noble one, and he entered it as it stood.

Then he audited his vote, since he owed that.

He had been asked what he would do, and he had answered for himself, and he had said that it should count for one hand. All of that was correct. It was also true that four people had heard him want something for the first time in two years, and that three of them were of an age to be moved by it. He had seen the researcher shut her mouth on a question. He had seen the big one look at him as he looked at a load that had shifted. Had he put his thumb on the scale? He thought not. The boy had ruled on his own ground, and the boy's reason was better than his: it was about what the paper did not know, and it would have stood if Seln had never spoken.

He was not certain. He wrote that down too, in the place where he kept such things, which was nowhere.

There was a way to be certain, and he had been looking at it since the fire was banked.

The paper followed the case. He had said so himself. If the case were to go south tonight, by itself, on one man's chest, then by first light there would be nothing at this fire that anybody in the west had priced. Five people would wake to a walk at fifty and a fence at forty. He knew the south road; he had walked the first hours of it alone in their second week here, as he had walked every road out of this town. He could be on the ridge before the stars turned, and he would have his face, very likely; and an arm two weeks old would never come within a mile of that floor.

His hand was on the strap, and he let it stay there, and looked at the thing squarely.

He had lost an argument on a shelf above a valley with no name, on the second night past the Line. He had proposed that the watches should be his to arrange as he judged best, since he had fifteen years of judging them; and a girl with a ruled table had told him that the rota was not set aside for anybody's judgment, his own included. He had been angry for an hour and grateful ever since. A man who has decided things alone for fifteen years does not stop because he has resolved to; he stops when four people will not let him.

And there was the other reason, which he had given a boy in a loft. He had told him that he would sooner see a healer warned of what might be done to her than be used as the reason for keeping her ignorant. If that was good for a healer, it was good for a crew. He could not carry the danger off in the night and leave them to find a cold place under a tree. That was the service's way. It had been his own way, every week, for fifteen years: to know what was best for a subject, and arrange it, and say nothing.

He had resigned from that. He had merely never filed it.

Four hands had gone up at that fire, and one had stayed down, and had asked to be entered, and was going regardless. He was not entitled to take a decision out of five people's hands by removing its object, however kindly he meant it.

He took his own hand off the strap.

The case lay where it had lain, against his breastbone, with its lock toward him. He knew what was in it to the leaf. He had not opened it since a reading room above Ostrand, and he did not open it now; there was nothing in it that would tell him who was waiting at the foot of a ridge, and there was one leaf in it that was not a copy.

The stars had turned as far as they turned in one watch, and he got up, without a sound, and went round the four sleepers, and the boxes, and the mule, as he had gone round every camp of theirs since the shelf. Then he bent and touched the girl's right shoulder with two fingers. He chose that shoulder with care.

She woke at once, as she did everything.

"Anything?" said Lira.

"Nothing," said Seln.

It was the report that he gave at the end of every watch. She took the blanket that he held out, and put her back against the ash, and he lay down where she had been lying, which was still warm.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-07-void-roads/manuscript/chapter-47.md

# Chapter 47 — Millrace

They were out of Lowmarch two hours before the light on the hundred and thirteenth day, with the stars still hard over the river and every blade in the meadow standing white.

It was Seln's hour, and Cael had given it to him without argument. A loaded cart took the whole of a short day to reach the mill; a crew that walked as they walked could be on the ridge by sunrise and at the fork before noon, and Seln wished to have the afternoon in hand. So Brom loaded the mule by feel. Lira ate standing. They went past Pike's bare plank and the smith's cold chimney, and took the south road in file, with the frost ringing under five pairs of boots and four small hooves.

They passed the well at the fork in the dark. It had a new rope on its windlass, and somebody had mended the curb where six stones had been set; and Cael, who had hauled foul air out of that shaft for three days on a bitten leg, put his hand on the stonework as he went by.

The road climbed from there. By the time there was grey enough to see by they were on the back of a long ridge, bare and broad, with pale grass on it that the wind had combed all one way. The country fell off on both hands. To the east it was hills beyond hills, hatched with the red-barked trees that Karis would not name. To the west the ground went down in long folds toward a valley that he could not yet see into, and a line of mist lay along the bottom of it like wool caught on a fence.

"That's water," said Lira. "Under the white. It's the same that she walked down."

"The mill water," said Karis. She had the calfskin half unrolled against her chest, and she was reading it as she walked. "He draws it with three strokes where it is forded and with one where it is not. It comes round out of the hills to the north and east. A person leaving that hold by its northern wicket would meet it on the second morning, and it would bring her here."

The sun came up on their left hand, small and white. It showed them their own breath, and the mule's, and a road that ran on south along the ridge as far as they could see; and a little before the middle of the forenoon it showed them a place where the ruts divided.

One pair kept the ridge. The other turned down the western face, slantwise, through a notch that wheels had worn, and went out of sight.

"The fork," said Seln. He had walked the first hours of that road by himself, in their second week at Lowmarch, and had never said what he found. "The left hand is ours, later. The right is the mill."

They heard the wheel before they saw a roof.

It came up to them out of the notch while they were still among the rocks: a slow heavy knocking, with water falling under it, at about the pace of a tired man splitting wood. Brom stopped the mule to listen. Then the track came round a shoulder, and the valley opened.

It was a narrow green place, a good deal greener than anything on the ridge. The water came down it fast and brown over a weir of stakes and stone, and above the weir a channel had been cut away along the bank to a tall grey building with a wheel on its side as high as a barn door. The wheel went round steadily in a white fall of water. There was a fence of split posts about the mill and a dozen roofs, and below the fence a ford, with a flat stone on each bank for the wheels to aim at. Smoke rose straight up from four chimneys.

A woman was sitting on the nearer of the flat stones with a net of some kind across her knees. She raised her head at the sound of hooves, and counted them down the track with her chin, one at a time, as Pike counted a meadow. Then she turned and called toward the mill, without getting up, in a voice that had been pitched to carry over a wheel.

"Five off the ridge."

The answer came from a lean-to that had been built against the mill's southern wall, where the sun fell; and it came at once, low and hoarse, from somebody who had not troubled to come to the door.

"I know."

---

There was a bench along the mill's wall, and they sat down on the end of it.

She had told him at a well that if she was at a table when he found her, he would sit and wait his turn like anybody; and he had not understood, until he had the planks under him, what a plain relief it would be to do a thing exactly as it had been set. Nobody on that bench looked at him twice. There were fifteen people on it, or had been at first light; nine were left, and two more stood about the door with their caps in their hands.

The lean-to had no front. It was three walls of board and a roof of turf, with the mill's warm stone for its back; she had her plank on two kegs in the middle of it, and her things laid out along a shelf as they had lain on a sill at Thornwater. He could see the whole of her work from where he sat, and he watched it as he had once watched a sixth-year's bout from a rail.

She had a man on the plank when they came, with his right hand in her two.

It was a bad hand. He was one of the miller's men, and a rope had taken it nine days before, against the drum of the hoist that lifted the sacks; it had been bound up in a meal-bag since, and it lay in her palms like a thing that had been trodden on. She had her eyes shut. Her lips were moving, and Cael found that he knew where she was by the count: at three she had the banks, and by seven she had gone along the bed to where it was stopped. He had lain under those hands for twenty minutes. He had a fragment in him of what she was doing, the tenth, and it sat quiet under his breastbone and did not stir toward her, since it stopped at the length of his own arm.

She had been at it for most of an hour before they came, by the grey of the man's face, and she held it a quarter of an hour longer. When she laid it down on the man's chest, it had five fingers that he could move, and he was weeping without any noise.

"That's one," said Oryn. "You'll not close it on a rope before the thaw. Send in the child."

It was a girl of three or four, carried in by her father, with a wet rattling breath that Cael could hear from the bench. The healer did not put her on the plank. She sat down on a keg and took the child on her own knee, with one broad hand spread on the small back and the other on the breast, and bent her cropped head until her ear was against the shoulder-blades. She stayed so for a long time.

He saw her face over the child's hair. There was another chest, at another gate, that she had last listened to on the eighty-ninth evening. She had counted the days to it on a hill, aloud, with nobody by. None of that showed. She had one child under her hands, and she gave it the whole of herself, as she gave everything.

"It's low down, and on the one side," she said to the father at last. "It's not what you were afraid of. Hold her so. She'll cough, and you're to let her."

The mending of that took a good deal less time than the hand, and at the end of it the child coughed until she was red, and then slept.

"That's two."

The third came in on a hurdle.

She was an old woman, very small and very upright, with two grandsons to carry her and a stick laid along her side that she plainly meant to walk home on. Her left knee was the size of a loaf under her skirt. The healer looked at it from her stride and a half, and then at its owner, and something came into her tired face that Cael had seen there only once before, at a board with a goat on it.

"You were to wait for me on your own doorstep. I'd put a day by for it."

"I waited sixteen of them," said the old woman. "Then I heard you were at the mill. I'd sooner be carried down a valley than sit another night with it."

"What did you do?"

"Nothing. I was getting a ham down."

"Off a ladder." Oryn had her hands round the joint already. "It's got a ladder in it. I can feel every rung. You went through the fourth, and you came down on this with the rest of you after."

"The third."

"It was the fourth. Lie back."

That one took her until the shadow of the wheel lay across the door, and when it was done she sat on her keg with her head down between her shoulders for as long as it takes to count fifty.

"That's three," she said to the floor. "That's the day."

She had told them on a mountain that her reserve was a purse, and that three such mendings emptied it.

She did the rest of them with one hand each, and that took her less time than the knee had. Cael counted a dozen. There was a carter with a wrenched back, and a boy with a stye, and a woman whose tooth she looked at and would not touch. She laid two fingers on a wrist, or a palm on a forehead, for a count of ten, and told each of them the truth in a sentence. She put tape on a thumb. She told a big sullen man that there was nothing wrong with him that a week away from his brother would not cure, and the whole bench laughed, the man included.

Then there was nobody between the five and the door.

---

"You," said Oryn.

She was speaking to him. She had washed to the elbow in a bucket, and she stood drying her hands on her breeches, a stride and a half inside the lean-to, with the old woman's hurdle going away up the valley behind her.

He got up off the bench and went to the edge of the turf roof's shadow, and stopped there.

"How many?"

"One. The same one. I've not gone back to it."

"Then you've nothing for me yet, and you know it, and you sat an hour on my bench all the same." Her eyes went over him as they had gone over him in a yard. "So it's something else. Say it."

He had turned it over on the ridge since before the sun was up, and it had seemed a plain enough thing there. It was harder in front of her, with her sleeves pushed back and three mendings gone out of her since first light.

"I've read one thing with what I had from you, and it was myself. I found no bank in it anywhere, as you found none. I don't know what a bank is like under the hand. I've only your word that there is such a thing." He kept his own hands at his sides. "I'd go back to it better if I had felt one. So I'm asking whether I may read you. Here, or afterward, or on a day you name. The short one, and no more than you choose."

He heard Lira's breath stop, on the bench behind him.

The healer did not answer at once. He saw her look at his right hand, where it hung, and then at her own wrist; and for the space of one breath there was the hunger in her face that he had seen at a ford and in a yard, when she had wanted to know a thing more than she wanted her supper. She put it by as he had seen her put it by before.

"No."

She said it without heat. She hung the cloth on its nail, and turned back to him.

"You asked. That's the rule and you kept it. The answer's no. Next."

He stood for as long as it took him to be sure that he had nothing to add, and he had nothing. He had asked a person for the first time in his life whether he might do a thing to her; she had said that he might not; and the whole of what was left to him was to go and sit down. He went and sat down. It was not hard to do, and he was a little surprised at that, since he had dreaded it.

Lira was getting to her feet beside him, with her right hand already at the knot of her sling.

He took Hesk's book out of the satchel. He had written nothing in it yet that day, and he wetted the pencil and put this down before anything else, with the date over it, so that it should stand first.

*Hundred and thirteenth day. Millrace, the healer's bench, a little after noon. Asked her whether I might read her. It is the first time that I have asked anybody. She said no. Entered before anything else today, since it is the first of its kind and I want it to head the leaf.*

Karis had been reading it upside down, from his other side.

"She looked at your hand before she said it," Karis murmured. Her voice was pitched for his ear alone. "And then at her own wrist. I have seen that look on a person in front of a shut press. She was curious about you. Enter that somewhere."

"It's not mine to enter."

"It is an observation. You may put my initial against it."

He put it down, with her initial; and he had hardly lifted the pencil when a hoarse voice from the lean-to said that the arm had taken no harm on two days of frozen road, that all eight were as they had been, and that the knot had been retied by somebody who tied like a carter.

"That was me," said Brom, from the bench. "At the ford. She'd got it wet."

"I can see who it was. Hand."

He gave her his hand across the sill, and she turned it over and gave it back. She put two fingers on Karis's throat. She gave Seln her short nod. In the time that it takes to draw a bucket she had gone round the five of them and found nothing that wanted her, and she sat down on her keg with her forearms on her knees.

"That's my bench, then. Which of you is going to tell me why you're a day south of your board, with a mule of mine and three boxes, on a road that goes nowhere but a ford?"

---

Cael told her, since it was his to tell.

He gave it in order and left nothing out: a line that was said twice at a hearth, and a nail, and a sheet of cream paper that had lain sealed in a broker's box beside the first. He gave her the sum on it. He told her what Seln had found in its margin, and what the five of them had made of it at a fire, and that one of the five had said walk away and been entered. He said that the place was half a day up the ridge from the fork over her head, and that he meant to be at its edge by dusk and across it in the morning.

"You said not without you. So I've not come to ask you for anything. I've come so that you should have it from me today, with the bench done, and not from a carter in a fortnight."

She heard all of it with her eyes on her own hands. When he had finished she spread the left one on her knee, and began to fold the fingers down.

"I was to be at this gate on the ninety-sixth. I came in on the hundred and twelfth. That's sixteen." The thumb went down. "There's a day's bench here still, that I'd have done tomorrow, and the road beyond it: two gates, and then the ford with the oxen on the hundred and twenty-first, where they looked for me on the hundred and fourth. That's seventeen." A finger. "Now put your ridge into it. It's a half-day up to wherever this is, and I'd not see the place before dark. A day while you're in it. A half-day down. That's two, if every one of you walks out on his own legs, and you've not once done that since I knew you." Another finger. "So the ford's nineteen. And beyond the ford there's a child that I heard on the eighty-ninth and can't hear now before the hundred and twenty-eighth."

She looked at the two fingers that were left standing.

"You're asking me to break the route," she said.

"I'm not asking. I'm telling you where we'll be."

Her head came up. She studied him for some time, and he could see her going back over the words, as he had seen her go back over a count that came out odd.

"That's worse. That's a healer's sentence."

"I had it from one."

"I know where you had it. I said it to a loft, with my hand on a ladder." She rubbed her palms together slowly. "It's what I say at every gate when I leave it. I don't ask them whether I may go. I tell them where I'll be, and then it's theirs to carry, and they can't say afterward that they weren't told. I never had it said to me before. I don't care for it."

"I'd not have known another way to put it that wasn't asking."

"No. There isn't one." She got up off the keg. "You've a girl there with a fortnight's arm, and you're taking her onto a floor that somebody chose for her. I said that somebody should stand outside with her sleeves up. I meant it when I said it. I didn't think that I'd be shown the place inside three days."

She walked to the lean-to's edge and stood looking up the valley, where a hurdle was still in sight, very small, between two boys.

"I'll say one thing, so that it's been said before any other. I'm not going to be anybody's sixth."

"Nobody's said it."

"That one-armed man said it under his eave, and wrote it. A clerk said it to a boat." She did not turn round. "I've a round. It's seven gates and twenty days and it's the only thing that I own. A woman who's one of six belongs to the six. I've seen what that does to a healer; she ends up mending the same four people for the rest of her days and calling it a calling."

She stood there for about as long as it takes to count twenty. Cael did not help her, and nor did any of the others; it was her sum, on her fingers, and they had each of them learned at a stone that she did not want it done for her.

Then she put two of those fingers into her mouth and whistled.

A man came round the corner of the mill with a sack on his shoulder. He was a carter by his whip, long and stooped, and he had a cart at the ford half loaded with meal for the gates up the water.

"You go north tomorrow," said Oryn. "You'll say this at every fence you stop at, in these words, and you'll have it said on to the ford with the oxen. The healer's late. She was seventeen at the ford, and she's nineteen now, or more. She's a half-day south of the mill, at the edge of bad ground, since there's a crew gone onto it that she's had her hands in." She waited until his lips had done moving. "And at the gate beyond the ford, where the little one is: if the breathing changes, they're to send down the water to this mill, and the mill sends up the ridge. I'll come from wherever I am."

"Late, nineteen, bad ground south of the mill," said the carter. "And the little one sends here."

"That's it."

He shifted his sack and went back to his cart. She watched him go, and Cael saw what he had seen on her face in a yard, when she unloaded a mule: it was the look of a person who has done the only thing that could be done from where she stood, and who knows to a grain how much less that is than enough.

---

"Now you'll hear what it is," she said, "since you'll want it for your book, and I want it plain."

She had come back to the middle of the lean-to. She did not sit.

"I'm not coming with you. I've put you on the round. That's a different thing, and I'll not have it mistaken." Her eyes went along the five of them, and they were as level and as tired as he had ever seen them. "You're on the route now. Which means when I say lie still, you lie still, and when I say I can't help you, you believe me."

"That's the whole of it?" said Brom.

"That's what every gate gets. They feed me, and they do as they're told on my plank, and when I tell them that a thing's past my hands they don't stand there looking at me as if I'd kept something back." She glanced at Cael. "You've stood there so. On a hillside, with her arm in my lap. I'll not have it a second time."

"You won't," said Cael.

"And I go when I go. The day that the round can't spare me, I turn north, and nobody says a word."

"I'll not ask you to stay. I'll write that down."

"Write it where you wrote the other."

It was Lira who spoke next, and she did it from the bench, with her foot set down whole and her chin up.

"We lay six bowls. We started at the stone, and we never got out of it; Brom puts six out every night and takes one back." She held the healer's eye. "Nobody's asking you to be a thing. I only thought you ought to know that there's a bowl."

Oryn regarded her for the space of a breath.

"I'll eat out of it," she said. "I'll not sit in a place."

"That's all I said."

"I know what you said. You said it a deal better than he would have." The corner of her mouth went down, which with her was most of the way to a smile. "Get that mule off my fence-post. She's eaten the bark."

They were half an hour getting away, since a healer does not leave a gate as a crew leaves a camp. She went into the mill and came out with a loaf and a cheese that somebody had pressed on her. She showed the miller's wife how the hand was to be bound at night. And then she went round to the back of the cart-shed with thirty feet of three-strand rope over her shoulder, and what she brought back on the end of it made Brom put his hand over his mouth.

It was a jenny mule, mouse-grey and enormous in the belly, with ears like a pair of oars. She came round the corner of the shed sideways, with her head stuck out and her eyes rolling at the wheel, and it took the healer three tries to get her past it.

"They've had her standing since the barley was in," said Oryn, a little breathless. "She's eaten her head off and learned nothing. The miller says he'll thank me to take her as far as the snow." She threw her one pannier across the grey back and buckled it. "She leads as a gate swings. I've told them so on thirty rounds."

The brown mule had turned her head.

She stood under her three boxes at the fence-post, quite still, with both ears pricked at the square woman in the dead man's coat. The healer did not go to her. She tightened a strap on the grey that did not want tightening.

"She knows you," said Brom.

"She's a mule. She knows where the hay used to come from." Oryn took up the rope. "You'll keep on the low side of her, as I told you. I'll go behind with this one, where she can't see the load and want it."

They forded below the mill, and the woman on the flat stone counted six of them and eight hooves across without lifting her net; and they climbed out of the green valley into the wind again.

At the fork there was a flat rock, and Cael sat down on it with Hesk's book while the two mules were let to blow. The grey had come up the notch backward for a good part of the way. The mill's wheel was still to be heard below them, very small. He wrote under what he had written on a bench.

*Hundred and thirteenth day, the fork above Millrace. Oryn came. On her terms. She'll turn north the day she has to and I won't ask her not to.*

He read it over. Then he looked at six people on a bare ridge, and at the left-hand ruts, which ran on to the south with the white sun over them.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-07-void-roads/manuscript/chapter-48.md

# Chapter 48 — Two Stakes

"Well?" said Oryn.

She had called the halt herself, an hour south of the fork, where a grey rock as big as a cart stood up out of the ridge and broke the wind. The two mules were nose to tail in its lee. Brom had the bread out. She had sat down on a ledge of the rock with her coat pulled round her, and had looked at Cael across the trodden grass for as long as it takes to count ten before she spoke.

He knew what she meant, though he had been turned away at a bench three hours before, in six words, and had gone and sat down, and it had not once crossed his mind since that the matter might be opened again by her.

It was hers to open; he saw that, and he saw also that if he was to have it, he must put the whole question over again from the beginning, as though the first had never been answered, since she would not have him take a thing that he had not asked for.

"May I read you?"

[[TIER-B: Oryn's answer on the road — the map's "Yes. That's the answer, and I'm the one giving it…" — coordinator to supply protected text]]

Lira had stopped chewing, and Karis, who had her notebook half out of her coat, pushed it back in.

"I'd three mendings just gone out of me at that bench, and eleven people looking on," said Oryn. "You'd have put your fingers on a tired woman who was being stared at, and gone off thinking that was a Tide. I told you what a first reading is. It's the reader's morning. I'd not have yours spoiled by mine."

"And now?"

"Now I've eaten, and walked an hour, and there's nobody here who hasn't seen me asleep." She pushed back her left cuff. "And there's a reason for it, which there has to be. You said it yourself, and you said it well enough. You've read one thing in your life, and it's got no banks, and you don't know what one is. You can't tell what's missing from a thing until you've had your hands on one that's whole." Her eyes were level. "That's what it's for. It's not to learn whether your fingers work. I'd not sit here for that."

"I know that you wouldn't."
