"Then come here. You'll do it as I do it, since it's the only way you've seen. Two on the wrist. Two on the neck, under the jaw, where the beat is. You count to ten with your lips and you take your hands away at ten, whatever you've got."

He went to her. She had her wrist held out, inside upward, as he had seen a hundred people hold theirs out to her; it was broad and brown, with a white scar across the heel of the hand that he had never been near enough to notice. He laid the first two fingers of his right hand on it, and put two of his left under the angle of her jaw, lightly, where a pulse knocked.

It was strange beyond telling to stand so. She had stopped a stride and a half from every person that he had ever seen her meet, until her hands were wanted; and he had been read by her six times and had never once touched her.

"Breathe out," said Oryn. "You're holding it. Start when you've none left."

He let it go, and under his breastbone the tenth fragment, the lightest thing that he carried, turned toward his fingers, as a person turns her head at her own name.

*One.*

It went out through his right hand and into her wrist as it had gone out of his palm in a loft, blindly, like a stone dropped down a well. In himself it had come home to his own two fingers after the whole length of him. Here it came to his left, under her jaw, almost before he had counted.

*Two. Three.*

There was something there.

He nearly took his hands away. In himself, at ten, he had had only his own gait, quick and slow, and nothing whatever to say what it went along in. This went along *in* something. He felt the sides of it first, close on either hand, and high, and as plain as the banks of a mill-race where the stone has been laid by somebody who knew his work; he could have walked along the top of them.

*Four. Five. Six.*

And he knew it. That was the thing that he had not been ready for. Nobody had ever told him how a healer learned a Path from a count of ten, and he had supposed that it was a sort of reckoning, with rules, which took years. It was no more a reckoning than knowing a friend's step on a stair. Those banks had a shape, and the shape was hers, and it was a kind; and the name of the Path was in his mouth before he had asked for it.

*Seven. Eight. Nine.*

"Tide," he said, at ten, and took both hands away.

---

"Well, I'd hope so," said Oryn. "I've been one for eleven years."

She was rubbing her wrist with her other thumb, where his fingers had been, and she was watching his face with an attention that he had seen her give only to a thing on her plank.

"It was *there*," said Cael. He heard his own voice, and it was not steady. "At three. I'd counted to ten on myself and had my own feet and nothing else. I thought that was what ten gave you."

"It's what ten gives you on a thing with no sides to it. I had it five times off you, and I'd never had it off anybody." She nodded slowly. "So now you've felt both. That's worth an hour of my telling."

"What did it cost you?"

"Nothing that I'd notice. A count of ten never does." She considered him. "You've gone the colour you went on my table. Sit down."

He sat down on the turf at her feet, since his knees had some views of their own on the matter, and his right hand felt as though he had been holding it in a stream.

She was silent for a little while, and behind him the grey mule was trying to eat Brom's sleeve, and being told in a low reasonable voice that it was not hay.

"You'll have a minute of the long one," she said. "No more. I'll count it, and I'll say when, and you'll come out on my word and not on your own."

"You said that it wasn't run on a road."

"I said that I don't run it on a road, since it costs me a mending and I've none to throw about. It's you that's running this, and I'm only sitting under it." She was turning her collar down. "And a minute's no reading. It's a look through a door. But the short one shows you that there's a bed, and it never yet showed anybody what was running in it. I'd not have you go back to yourself knowing the half."

She turned round on the ledge, so that her back was to him.

"Your left hand on the back of my neck, under the collar. The right stays on the wrist." She held it out behind her. "It goes along from the one and comes home to the other. You know how it goes. You've had it done."

He got up on his knees. He laid his left palm on the back of her neck, under the cropped hair, where her own left hand had once lain under his for twenty minutes, and took her wrist again; and he felt her draw one long breath and let the whole of it go, as she had taught him to.

"Still and whole and willing," said Oryn, to the rock in front of her. "That's the three. Go on."

He let it go out, and went with it.

It was the banks again at first, close and high, and he went along between them at his own uneven pace. Then he was past the place where a count of ten would have stopped him, and for the first time he was *in* a person who was not himself.

There was one channel.

He had not known that he expected more until he found the one. In himself there were ten things going ten ways, each running into the next like water into water; he had lain in a loft and failed to find where any of them ended. Here there was a single bed and a single thing in it, which was deep, and did not wander.

And it ran.

That was the difference, and it took him some of his minute to find the word for it. His own went along, quick or dragging, as he was glad or tired, and it had never seemed to him to be going anywhere in particular. Hers was going somewhere: it ran as a river runs in its last mile, when it can smell the sea. Wherever his attention rested on it, he found it already leaning that way, toward his left hand, and then toward his right; it came to meet whatever touched it. It did not pool in any place, and there was no slack water in her at all. The whole of that woman, from the neck to the heels, was on its way *to* something, and had been for so long that the banks had worn to fit.

"Forty," said Oryn's voice, a long way off. "You're helping. Don't."

He eased his neck. He had seen a man's shin close under those hands in an hour, and had watched her walk to a mule and unload it, for five days that she could not spare; and he had taken her for a person who gave things up. She gave nothing up. She was made as a road is made, to get to where the hurt was, and everything in her that was not road had been spent on it years ago.

"Sixty. Out."

He took his hands away, and the going-along came home through his right hand and lay down, and he sat back on his heels.

She turned round on the ledge and put her collar up; she asked him nothing, and he understood that she did not mean to. She had sat in front of a great many people who had just been told what was in them, and she knew what it was to have the telling pulled out before it was ready.

It was Cael who spoke first after all, and it was not what he had meant to say.

"You're built to arrive."

It was only the truest thing that he had brought back.

"Yes," she said. "That's what I am."

She said it without any pride, and without the least surprise, as she might have agreed that her coat was brown. Then she got up off the rock and went to take the grey mule's rope out of Brom's hand.

Cael stayed where he was a little longer, with his right hand lying on his knee like something that he had borrowed, and he was as hungry as he had been after a day under barley.

"He asked twice and got one," said Lira, to nobody in particular.

"That's the rate," said Brom, and gave him the rest of the loaf.

---

They came to the end of the ridge with the sun a hand above the western hills, and the ground opened under them.

Cael stopped where the road tipped over, and counted what there was, as he counted anything that he would have to walk into.

The ridge did not run down into a valley. It broke off, as a table breaks off at its edge, and its western face went down in one long slope of pale grass to a low place that lay below them like the print of an enormous thumb. It was a bowl, though so shallow that a cart might have been driven anywhere in it. He put it at five hundred yards from side to side, which was far wider than the swell that the Fallow Ring had stood on, and it had not half that swell's height.

The carters' ruts did what he had seen them do once before. Halfway down the slope the ground eased into a kind of brow, and at the brow the live ruts swung away to the north and went round the whole of that low place, keeping their distance from it, and came back to their line on the far side. He could see them all the way, two pale threads on the grass, and inside them an older road went straight on down, grown over, with a hundred years of turf in it.

At the bottom lay the floor.

It was the same grey, and he knew that grey now as he knew the colour of his own hands. It lay in the lowest part of the bowl, round, and a good deal bigger than the other had been, and the low sun slid over it without finding anything to catch on. There was no ring of stones, and not an upright thing on it of any kind; it was as bare as a threshing floor.

"There's a hole in it," said Lira.

She had the best eyes among them, and he followed her finger, and found it: a small square of dark, exactly in the middle, cut so true that it looked like a thing drawn with a rule.

"And steps. On this side of it, going down away from us. I can see three, with the light on them." She shaded her eyes with her one hand. "Then it's black."

"A stair," said Brom.

"It's what he calls the road," said Karis. Her voice was very small. "It is on no map that I own, and every carter in that meadow has known for a hundred years what is at the end of it."

Nobody moved, and the wind had dropped; in all that low ground there was not a bird, or a beast, or any smoke; there was a bare grey floor with a square cut in it, and three steps that somebody had made for feet.

Seln went down first, as he went down everything: he walked the old road to the brow and stood there; then he went along the live ruts to the north until he was small, and came back, and went a little way south. When he returned to them his face told nothing.

"There are runs in the grass, on the north side. They are old. Nothing has used them since the frosts began." He glanced at the healer. "I should have looked for a den, and I find none from outside. I do not know why, and I shall not guess. On the ruts there is no mark newer than a salt cart's, a week old."

"So nobody's here," said Brom.

"Nobody has been on the ruts. I have said nothing about anywhere else."

They brought the mules down to the brow. Karis gave Brom her box-rope to hold, and went on by herself down the old road toward the floor, with her arms a little out from her sides, counting under her breath. Cael walked a stride behind her and to one side, and he had no need to count, since he was watching the back of her neck.

At forty paces she stopped as if she had come to a wall. Her sigil had gone out at that stride, as it had gone out on the Fallow.

She stood for one breath. Then she stepped backward, a single stride, and let her shoulders down; and she bent, and picked a stone out of the old rut, grey and as big as a loaf, and set it on the turf at her feet.

"Here," she said. "The edge of the Quieting. It is the same as the other; there is no warning in it at all."

One by one they came down and tried it, since no one of them would take another's word for such a thing: Lira, and Brom, and Seln, each a stride past the stone and a stride back. The healer went over last, with two fingers on her own wrist and her lips moving, and came out again and nodded. Cael walked across beside Brom, and felt what he had felt on the Fallow, which was grass under his boots.

Karis was looking down the old road to the floor, and then at the square in the middle of it, with her head on one side.

"Two hundred. I'll pace it in the morning."

---

It was Lira who found the iron.

She had gone a few steps along the line to the north, with her eyes on the turf, as she went along any new ground; and she stopped with the toe of the great boot against something that stood a hand's length clear of the grass.

"There's your bar," she said. "And it's got a number on."

It was the same pattern as the one that Cael had knelt by on the ninety-sixth day, a cord north of a grey stone: a square bar as thick as two fingers, with its head beaten down by a mallet. This one was scaled all over with rust, in leaves, so that it looked more like a thing grown than a thing made. A thumb's breadth under the head there was the registry's stamp, and under the stamp, punched deep, were two figures.

"Thirty-two," said Lira. She did not crow; she said it as she would have read out the height of a wall. "I said that there'd be one."

"You said *like as not*," said Karis. "I shall enter that you said it, and the day."

"Enter that I was right."

"I shall enter the figure. It will do the same work."

Seln had gone down on one knee, and he did not touch the bar; he looked at the scale on it, and at the way the turf had closed about its foot, and then he turned his head, slowly, and looked across the old road.

"There is another."

It stood on the south side of the grown-over ruts, eight feet from the first, with Karis's stone between the two. It was the same bar, but the rust on it was thin and bright, a skin and not a bark, and the grass about its foot had not yet healed.

Seln went to it on his knees.

"That first has stood five cold terms. I said so of its fellow on the Fallow, and I see no cause to alter it." He bent closer. "This has had one. Two, at the most. And it is the same number. Thirty-two."

"Two bars and one number," said Lira. "So it's the place that's thirty-two. It's not the bar."

"That is an inference," said Karis, "and it is a fair one, and I am still not writing down what the number counts."

"There is something beneath it," said Seln.

He had not raised his voice, but Cael came and knelt beside him. Under the two punched figures, on the newer iron only, there was a mark that the old bar did not have. It was small and deep, and made with two blows of a different punch: a short upright stroke, and under it a bar laid across.

It was not a letter that he knew, and it was not a figure; and when he looked at Seln, Seln was looking at it as he looked at any paper, through and not at.

"Newer. This one's been re-surveyed."

"What's the mark?" said Brom.

"I do not know. I carried sheets, and I never saw it on a sheet." Seln got up, and brushed the frost from his knee. "I can tell you what a second bar is. Somebody came back to a place that was already measured, with the first iron standing in plain sight, and measured it again, and wished it known that he had. I cannot tell you why he came. I shall not put a meaning on two strokes of a punch."

Karis drew the mark in her notebook, at its own size, with the number over it, and wrote nothing beside it.

Cael stayed on his knees a breath longer than the others.

There were two bars of iron, eight feet apart, with a road going between them. Anchor had been asking at every sill and gate and rail for the better part of a year, and it asked now, in its thin held note: here was a doorway, and here were two posts to it, and would he not like something set across? He told it no, as he had told it at a stake on the Fallow. He left unexamined whatever might have been set there already, since he had no cause to examine it; and that refusal he was to go over more than once in the day that followed.

They made their camp on the outside of the stone, between the two irons, with the brow forty paces above them and the mules hobbled a little up the slope. Brom laid out six bowls, and he did it without looking at anybody, and the healer ate out of the last of them standing up.

She came by Cael afterward, at her stride and a half, with the grey mule's rope over her shoulder.

"You've your hand on your wrist."

He had. He took it off.

"Not tonight. I can see what you're thinking; you've had a bank under your fingers and you'd like to go and look for your own while you remember it." She shook her head. "You'd not be reading yourself. You'd be reading tomorrow, and calling it you. I said a night that's only a night. This isn't one."

"No."

"Then sleep, if that quiet one lets you."

---

The quiet one did not let any of them sleep until he had said his order, and he said it at the fire, when it was full dark, with the six of them close about it.

"I have walked the ruts," said Seln. "All the way round, at dusk. It is sixteen hundred paces and a little more. I found no person and no sign of one, and I shall walk it again before light. That is a fact about the ruts." He folded his hands. "If I had laid this, I should not be on the ruts tonight either. I should be a quarter of a mile back from them, on my face, in grass of this colour, and I should come up to them when you were over that stone and not before. So I shall tell you what I can see, which is nothing. And I shall tell you what I should assume, which is that they are here."

"All of that, and we go," said Brom.

"We ruled it at a fire. I am saying how."

He gave it to them a piece at a time, as he had once given five people the order of a watch.

"We go at first light. We go in file, ten yards apart, down the old road: myself, and then Lira, and Karis, and Cael, with Brom last. The bow is spanned before we cross, with a bolt in the groove, and Brom carries it. Each of us has a day's water and a day's bread on his back. I have known a morning's errand to take a day." His eyes went to the boxes. "Nothing else goes over. The three boxes stay at this stone with both beasts."

"My paper is in the middle one," said Karis.

"It will be there at noon. If the floor is as empty as it looks, you will come up for it yourself, and I shall carry it down." He turned to the healer. "You said that somebody should stand outside the line."

"I'll stand at that stone with my sleeves up. I said it, and I meant it." Oryn was sitting on her heels. "I've learned what my hands are worth on the far side of one. If a thing goes wrong in there, you bring it to me. It's two hundred yards. I've seen an arm carried as far."

"Then this stone is the objective, as the other was. Whatever is seen, whatever is heard, the word is *Back*, and every one of us comes to it by the straightest way." Seln looked at each of them in turn. "It is a good plan for a place with nobody in it. I do not know of a good plan for the other."

He looked at Cael last.

"I said a thing at the fire in the meadow, and I told you that I should say it again here. If there are hands, in the dark, at arm's length, you do as you think fit. If there are eyes, you are a boy with a knife. Nothing that a pair of eyes could carry to a boat."

"I heard it."

"I am saying it to five people now, so that when you stand still with all of us looking at you, four of them will know why."

Karis had got up while he was speaking. She went to the middle box and undid its cord, and lifted the lid a hand's breadth, and felt down the inside of it without a light. What she drew out was a single leaf. Cael knew it by its ruling, even by the fire: six rows, and a numeral struck through at the head of the sixth, and three lines of small exact writing against it.

She folded it once and put it inside her coat, against her shirt, and tied the box again.

"There is nothing else in there that says a word about him," she said, to nobody. "That one goes where I go."

He wrote the night's log with his back against the old iron.

*Hundred and thirteenth day, night. At the edge of the second one. A stone, and two bars, both numbered thirty-two; one five cold terms old, one a cold term or two, with a mark under its number that Seln won't read. A floor with no stones on it, and a hole, and a stair. Nobody on the ruts.*

*Tide: once, on the healer, on the ridge road. I asked twice today and she granted the second. The short count and then a minute of the long, at one sitting; I am counting that as one, since it was one asking and one yes. At three there were banks. I knew the Path by ten. In the minute, one channel and one thing in it, going somewhere. It cost her nothing that she would notice. It cost me the use of that hand for an hour, and most of a loaf.*

*She says not tonight for the other, and she is right.*

*Deployment since the Line: fourteen.*

He slept a little toward morning. When Brom woke him the stars were going out, and the frost lay so thick on the old road that it showed like a chalk line all the way down to the floor. Seln was coming back along the ruts from the north, and he shook his head at them from thirty yards away.

They ate cold. Brom put his boot into the stirrup of a healer's bow, and set the hook on the string, and straightened his back; the string came up the stock and dropped behind its catch, and he laid one of the five bolts in the groove. Karis had her lamp and her notebook and a flask, and Lira drew the knot of her sling tight with her teeth.

Then Seln stepped over the stone.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-07-void-roads/manuscript/chapter-49.md

# Chapter 49 — Forty Steps

A hawk over that place at first light on the hundred and fourteenth day would have seen three rings, one inside another, and a line drawn across them.

The outermost was the carters' road, two pale ruts going the whole way round the low ground. Forty paces inside it lay the edge that could not be seen, where a grey stone sat on the turf between two iron bars. And in the middle, two hundred and twenty yards from the stone, was the floor, as round as a coin, with a square hole at its centre no bigger than a cart. The line across all three was the old road. It came down off the ridge from the east, over the brow where the ruts turned aside, between the two bars, and straight on down the frosted grass to the floor's edge.

Six people stood at the stone. The sun was coming up behind them over the ridge, and their shadows went before them down the old road.

Nothing had changed in that low ground since they looked down on it at dusk. Seln had come in along the ruts not a quarter of an hour before, shaking his head; the frost lay unbroken on the old road as far as the floor, and the only marks that it would carry by noon were the ones that their own boots were about to make.

Seln was already over. He walked as he walked every approach, without haste, with his hands a little away from his sides and the strap of a locked case across his chest. When he had gone ten yards he lifted two fingers, and Lira stepped over the stone after him, on the whole sole of the great boot.

"Gone," said Karis, ten yards behind Lira.

She said it quite quietly, at the stride. She had crossed such an edge more often than any of them, and she had told Cael at the fire that she did not expect ever to grow used to it; but she did not break her pace.

He went fourth. He felt the turf under his boots and the cold on his face, and nothing else whatever; the place behind his breastbone was as dark on the far side of that stone as on the near. The ten things that he carried lay as they had lain. He had made up his mind in the night that he would ask none of them for anything this morning unless the roof fell in, and he walked down the old road ten yards behind a small straight back with his hands empty.

Brom came last. Cael heard him grunt at the stride, as he had grunted on the Fallow, like a man who has felt a door shut behind him. The healer's bow was on his left shoulder, spanned, with a bolt lying in its groove.

The healer herself had not moved; he glanced round once and saw her, square and brown against the sunrise, with her sleeves pushed up to the elbow and two mules and three boxes on the slope behind her.

He was facing the floor again when he heard the sound.

It was not loud: a short dull knock, like a mallet on a wet post, and after it a hiss of breath between somebody's teeth.

He turned.

Brom was five yards inside the stone, where he had taken one step that he had not meant to take, and he was standing very upright, with his head a little on one side, as he stood when Karis told him something that he could not at once believe. A hand's length of ash wood stood out behind his right shoulder, with three grey feathers on it.

"Ridge," he said.

Cael looked up the old road, into the sun, and had to put his hand over his eyes. On the brow, where the ruts turned north, there was a shape standing that had not been there when Seln walked the ruts in the dark. It stood forty paces above the stone. There was a second shape beside it.

Forty paces to the stone, and five yards beyond: that made forty-five. He knew that distance, since he had walked a mile once in front of a cart with a grey thing keeping it behind him in the grass, patiently, waiting upon a signal.

"Back," said Seln's voice, from far down the road.

It was the word, and all four of them came round to it as they had agreed. But the healer had not waited for a word. She was over the stone already, running, with her coat flying out behind her; she had seen a man hit five yards from where her hands worked, and she had come to fetch him out.

---

Cael ran too, and he was thinking as he ran.

Something was wrong with that shot, and though he had only fifteen yards to cover, it took him the whole of them to say what. A bow on that brow could have let all five of them walk down to the floor, and had them two hundred yards inside, with nothing over them and nowhere to go. It had chosen to shoot the last man instead, when he was five strides from the way out, with a healer standing in plain sight at the door.

A shot of that kind did not want them to go on. It wanted them to turn round, or it wanted somebody to come in.

He was twelve feet from the stone when he made up his mind. He had refused Anchor, the thinnest of his ten, at these two irons the evening before, and again in the frost an hour ago. He let it look now, on purpose, with his eyes open; and since he had stopped dead to do it, he stood on a frozen road for the length of two counts while a friend with a bolt in him was being turned about by the arm.

At twelve feet there was a greyness in the air between the bars, like breath. He took one stride more, and it was there.

Three lines were going across the old road.

They came from the old iron, on the north side, and they went to the newer one, eight feet away, at the height of a knee and a chest and a hand above a tall man's head. He saw them arrive, and they did not creep; they swung across as a gate swings to, all three together, and caught on the newer bar, and ran on past it to the south until they were beyond his seeing. On the north side they ran away from the old iron along the turf in the same three courses, and went out of his sight in that direction too.

He had seen such things once before, in a chalk cut on the Ostrand road, and he knew the craft as he knew his own handwriting. A point was set against a surface and told to stay, and a line was drawn from it to the next.

But these were not hers.

The woman in the cut had laid nineteen points round him while he fought her, each as fine as a fishing line, from fifteen feet, with the stillness of a person threading a needle. Whoever had laid these was forty paces off. And they were thick: every one was as thick as his wrist, and there were three where she would have used thirty. It was the same trade in a different hand, as a ship's cable and a stitch are the same trade: this hand threw.

"Stop," he said. "It's shut."

The healer did not so much as turn her head. She had Brom's left arm across her shoulders and she was bringing him up the road toward the stone at a stumbling run, with her eyes on the grey wool of his coat where a dark stain was beginning. She had three strides to go, and Cael saw her thinking of nothing in the world but those three strides, at the end of which she would be a Tide again.

"Oryn. *Stop.*"

She walked into it.

Her right shoulder met the middle course exactly as a shoulder meets a door that somebody has shut in the dark. She stopped as if she had been struck. Brom's weight came on past her, and his chest found it, and he made a sound that Cael did not wish to hear again; and the two of them stood swaying against nothing at all, a yard short of a grey stone, with the sun in their faces.

She put up her free hand. She laid the palm of it on the air, at the height of her eyes, and pressed.

Then she looked at the hand.

"Four," she said. Her lips had been moving. "I can't get past four."

"There's a fence across it. From that bar to that one, and on round. You can't see it. I can." He had her by the elbow. "It wasn't there when you came over. It went up behind you."

She understood him. He saw her understand all of it at once, as she understood a break under her hands: the bolt, and where it had been put, and for whom. Her face did not change. She shifted Brom's arm higher on her shoulder.

"That was for me, then," said Oryn. "That bolt."

Seln was beside them, having come back up forty yards of road without a sound, and Lira and Karis were behind him.

"They are standing up," he said.

---

Cael turned on his heel, slowly, all the way round, and counted what the morning had on its rim.

There were two on the brow to the east, where the old road came over. One had a crossbow, with its nose on the ground and his boot in the stirrup; he was drawing the string with a hook, steadily, like a man doing a thing that he had done ten thousand times. The other stood two yards south of him and had nothing in his hands at all, and his arms were coming down to his sides.

There were two on the southern rim, a quarter of the way round. They had risen out of the white grass behind the ruts while he was looking at the brow. One of them was kneeling over a bow, and the other only stood, a little apart, with his arms folded.

There was one on the west, a long way off beyond the floor, so small that Cael could tell only that it was upright and that it held something across its body.

And there was one on the north, kneeling.

That made six, and four of them had bows. Every one was the grey of the road in a dry month. They wore hoods, and under each hood there was a cloth drawn up across the face, of the kind that carters wore against dust. Not one of them had called out, or so much as lifted a hand to another. They stood at four stations round five hundred yards of grass, outside an edge that none of them had needed a stone to find, and they looked down.

"Six," said Seln. "Two at the gate, two south, one west, one north. Four bows. I walked past every one of those places twice."

"Can we rush the two?" said Brom. He was grey round the mouth, and his voice was quite steady. "It's forty yards."

"Through that?"

Brom looked at the air in front of his chest.

Nine fragments had come up in Cael at once.

He had not called them. They had risen as they had risen in a lane on the Fallow, each offering what it had. Wind was in his feet, and though it could not take him through three courses of that fence, it could take him a long way along the inside of it, faster than a hooded man could run round the ruts. Storm, the heaviest of them, lay ready; there was a rut at his feet, running straight up the hill to the brow, with seed on the grass at either lip. Ember had fire for his hand, and there was a great deal that a boy could do from where he stood, in a place where four of his friends could do nothing.

And six pairs of eyes were on him, from four sides.

*Nothing that a pair of eyes could carry to a boat.*

He held them. He stood on a frozen road, with a bolt in Brom's shoulder and a healer staring at her own hand, and refused nine things one after another in the only country on the continent where every one of them would have answered. He had done as much once before, on a white floor, with a girl's arm in a hound's mouth. It was no lighter. Seln had told him that it would not be.

Lira was looking at him.

She had seen it on the Fallow from a foot away, and she saw it now from three yards. He watched her know what he was doing, and why, and what it was going to cost somebody before the day was out. Then her eyes left his face, and went up to the brow, where a man had got his string behind its catch and was reaching to his belt for a bolt. They went south, and west, and north, right round, in the time of one breath.

"He's nearly spanned," she said. "And there's not a yard of this grass that one of them can't see."

Her gaze had come down the old road to the floor, and stopped.

"There's one place."

She did not wait to be answered. She stepped in close under Brom's good arm, and with her right hand she lifted the healer's bow off his left shoulder by its stock, spanned as it was, with the bolt held in the groove under her thumb.

"I can't span it," she said to him. "I can carry it."

Then she ran.

She ran *down*. She went away from the stone, and from the two mules, and from the only ground on which a Path would answer, straight down the middle of the old road toward a bare grey floor with a hole in it. She ran on the whole of her foot, slapping, like a child in its father's boots; a healer had told her four days ago that a certain bone was her own again, and she was using it.

"After her," said Seln.

---

They went as a unit, since they had been one for a long time.

Karis was away first, with her coat held up in both fists. Brom ran. A bolt in the shoulder is not a bolt in the leg, and he went down that road like a bullock, with the feathers of the bolt nodding at his shoulder and the healer at his left hand, matching him stride for stride with her fingers hooked in his belt. Seln ran a yard to his right. Cael came last, since somebody must, and since if any of them fell he wished to be the one who saw it.

It was a hundred and ninety yards of grass. He had paced none of it, and he measured it now with his lungs.

Wind lay in his feet the whole way, asking. He ran on his own legs, as he had run before ever he knew that there was anything else to run on; and a boy who could have been at that hole in four breaths went down the hill at the pace of a hurt man, at the tail of his own crew, with his back to a spanned bow.

At sixty the bolt came.

He heard that one. It went over his right shoulder with a sound like cloth tearing, and he saw it strike, far ahead, on the grey of the floor between Karis and Seln. It did not stick. It skipped, as a flat stone skips on a pond, and went skating away across the plate with its feathers spinning.

"Two," said Seln, running. He had not turned his head. "Both from the gate."

Lira was on the floor. She had not slowed, and her boots made no sound on it at all.

Two more came out of the sky.

They were not aimed, or not as the first had been. Cael saw them only when they were falling, two dark strokes dropping steeply from the north and from the south. They had been lofted from better than two hundred and fifty yards by people who knew to a hair how far their bows would throw. One struck the plate ten feet from Brom and leapt up again, end over end, and the other came down beyond the hole and slid.

"Three. Four."

He was on the floor himself then. It was as cold through his boots as the other had been, and as silent, and thirty yards of it had never seemed so far. The hole was in front of him. It was two strides square, with an edge as sharp as a box's; and on its near side a step went down, and another, and a third, each of them white with frost and sunlight.

Lira's head was already below the floor. Karis went in after her. Brom took the first step sideways, with the healer under his arm, and the dark had them.

Cael looked back from the lip, since he was the last, and since it was his to know.

The six had not moved from their stations. On the brow a man was setting his boot in a stirrup for the third time. Nobody was running down the slope, and nobody was shouting. They had watched six people go into a hole in the ground, and it seemed that this was a thing that they had expected to see.

He went down.

Blade, in his right arm, which had come to him from a friend's sword, asked its one word at the first step, where the stone turned a sharp corner under his heel. *Edge?* He told it no, and it asked at the second. He counted the steps by its asking, since he could count them no other way. Below the third the light stopped as if it had been cut off with a knife, and he was going down into black by the feel of a wall on his left hand, at a run, behind the sound of other people's boots.

*Eighteen. Nineteen.*

Somebody cried out below him.

It was Lira's voice, and it was cut short. There was a scrape, and a thud of something soft against stone, and then her breath, loud and ragged in the dark.

"Keep on," she said, between her teeth. "I've got it. I've got the bow. Keep *on*."

*Twenty.* The air changed at that step. It had been the frost's air above; here it was still, and dry, and a good deal colder, as it is in a cellar that has been shut for a long time. He went down through it with the edges asking under his feet.

At the fortieth there was no forty-first. His boot came down on the level, and he walked into Seln's back.

---

"Stand where you are," said Seln's voice, close to his ear. "All of you. I am striking a light."

There was a scrape of steel, and a spark, and another. Karis's breath went in and out somewhere to the left. Cael knew what she was doing as well as if he had seen her. She had put out her hand in the dark to set a point on her own wick, as she had done every night of her life since she was fourteen; and there was no hand to reach with.

The tinder caught. Seln held it to the lamp, and the small brass thing took the flame and steadied. Nobody had spoken since the fortieth step, and the only sound in that place was six people getting their breath.

It showed them a room.

The room took their breathing and gave none of it back. It was colder than the frost above, with the cold of a thing that has not been warm in a very long time, and it smelled as the bottom of a dry well smells, of stone and of nothing else.

It was square, and Cael put it at ten strides each way. The floor was the same grey, level and seamless. So was the roof. He looked up, and it took him a breath to understand what he was looking at: the roof of that room was the underside of the floor that they had run across. There was no beam in it and no arch; it lay over them like a lid, a foot above Brom's head.

The walls were cut.

Karis made a sound in her throat. From the floor to the roof, on all four sides, the grey was covered with the fine sunk lines that had been round the feet of eleven stones on the Fallow: straight, and hooked, and crossing. They ran in bands, one above another, each as tall as a man's forearm. There was more of it on one wall of that room than on the whole of the other place.

And in the middle of the floor, where the lamp only just reached, something lay.

It was a dark oblong, the length of a man's arm, and it had a look of leather or tarred cloth about it, as a case has. From one end of it a pale line ran out along the floor toward the wall that they had come in by, and stopped.

"A cord," said Brom thickly.

"Nobody goes near that," said Seln.

He had spoken more sharply than Cael had ever heard him.

"It is what the paper says we were sent for. Six people brought us to this room this morning by a road that they had prepared, and that is lying in the middle of it. It has lain there a good while, by the dust. It will lie there until I have read the floor round it, with more oil than we have." He took the lamp out of Karis's hand and gave it to the healer. "That is for him. Use it."

He went back to the doorway.

It was a plain square opening in the wall, the width of the stair. Seln stood in it with his back to the room and looked up forty steps at a small square of blue.

"They can shoot the bowl," he said.

He was speaking to himself, as a man does who is taking an inventory. He put his hand on the stone of the door's edge, and then on the wall of the stair, and measured something with his eye.

"They can't shoot the stair."

"Why not?" said Lira. She was sitting on the lowest step with the bow across her knees and her left side turned away from the light.

"They are two hundred and sixty yards from that hole, on every side, and very little above it. A bolt that comes in at the top must come in nearly level. It strikes the wall or the upper steps." Seln turned round. "From the rim, nobody can see into this. Nobody can see so much as the fourth step. And anybody who comes to the edge of the hole to look down is a black shape on a bright square, to a person sitting where you are sitting, with that."

Lira looked at the bow.

"I've one in the groove," she said. "There's four more in the sleeve on his belt, and I can't span it."

"I can span it. I trust that I shall not need to." He took the leather sleeve from Brom's belt as he spoke, and laid it on the step beside her. "Go up six. Sit so that you can see the square and it cannot see you. If anything stands in it, do as the man on the gate-walk taught you."

"Shooting's stopping," said Lira.

She got up, with her jaw shut, and climbed six steps on her whole foot, and sat down again with the stock laid along her right knee. That was the thirty-fourth, counting from the top. Cael saw her set her boot on the step below, heel and toe together, and let her breath go out.

---

The healer had Brom on the floor by then, with his back against the wall beside the door.

She had cut his coat away from the shoulder with Seln's knife. Karis held the lamp where she was told. In its light the thing was plain enough, and Cael made himself look at it, since it was his crew's: the bolt had gone in behind, a hand below the top of the shoulder, and its iron head stood out in front, under the collar-bone, by the length of a thumb. There was not much blood. What there was came slowly.

Oryn put her two hands round the shoulder, above the wood and below it, and shut her eyes.

Her lips moved. They stopped. She opened her eyes and took her hands away, and for the length of one breath she looked at them as she had looked at them on the road, in front of nothing.

"No," she said, to the hands. "Well. I knew that."

Then she became somebody else, as Cael had seen her do once before, at the rim of a white floor, with a strip of her own shirt in her teeth. She took the lamp from Karis and held it close, and looked at the wound from in front and from behind, for a long time, without touching it. She laid two fingers on the inside of Brom's left wrist and counted, with her lips, to a good deal more than ten, and put her ear to his back.

"Breathe in. All the way. Does it catch?"

"No."

"Cough."

He coughed, and went greyer.

"Shut your hand. That one. Now open it." She watched the four dark lines go across and come back. "Is it cold? Can you feel me pinch?"

"I can feel you pinch."

She sat back on her heels.

"I can see what's wrong. I can't feel it and I can't touch it. Tape it. Leave the bolt."

"You'll not draw it?" said Seln, from the door.

[[TIER-B: Oryn, of the bolt, to Seln's question — the map's "the bolt's the plug", given from the middle of a sentence — coordinator to supply protected text]]

"Tape," said Lira's voice, from up the stair. A small hard roll came down the steps, one at a time, and lay on the floor in the light. "It's what I've left from the ankle. It's hers anyway."

Oryn had it in her hands before it stopped rolling. She laid the first strip round the shaft in front, close, and the second behind; she did it by lamp and by eye, as a carter's wife would have done it, and it took her a quarter of the time that it would have taken anybody else in that room. When she had done she pulled what was left of his coat across.

"You'll sit there. You'll not lean on it, and you'll not be brave." She wiped her fingers on her breeches. "I said in a loft that somebody ought to stand outside one of these with her sleeves up. I was outside. I'd my sleeves up. And I'm in here with a roll of tape, and that's a clean wound that I could shut in an hour on the far side of a rock." Her voice was low and quite even. "I came south for exactly this. Enter that."

"It's entered," said Cael.

"I know that it is. I wanted to hear myself say it."

She stood up with the lamp, and turned, and held it toward the stair.

"Now you. Come down here where I can see."

"There's nothing," said Lira.

"There's a sleeve on that sling that was grey this morning."

For a breath there was no answer. Then Lira came down the six steps, slowly, and into the light; and the wool across her breast was dark and wet from the elbow to the wrist.

"It was the twentieth step," she said. She was looking at the wall above the healer's head. "The ankle went. I'd the bow in my right hand, so I'd nothing to put out. And then it was out. I never told it to. It hit the wall, and it took me, the whole of me, and I stood up off it."

Not one of them repeated what she had been told about that arm, since they all knew it, and so did she.

The healer undid her own knot and drew the wool down, and two of the eight small marks had opened, the lowest of each row, and were bleeding steadily.

"The rest have held," she said, after a long look. "It's not gone through. It's opened where it was newest." She was already tearing a strip from the tail of Cael's shirt, which he had held out to her. "I can't shut that either. I can bind it, and I can tie it higher, and you can keep it against your chest until there's a sky over us."

She bound it. She tied the sling again, shorter, so that the hand lay almost at the collar-bone.

"Back up your six steps," said Oryn. "And if you put that arm out for anything again, I'll take the other one off you myself."

"Yes."

When Lira's boots had stopped on the stair, the healer looked into the lamp. It had held a finger and a half of oil when Seln lit it. There was a finger now.

She blew it out.

The dark came down on them like a cloth. Far up, at the head of forty steps, there was a small square of sky. Cael stood under a lid of grey stone and went over what the morning had left him. There were six of them below it, of whom two were bleeding. There were six on the rim. There were five bolts in a healer's bow and its sleeve. Of somebody else's there were three lying on the floor above, and a fourth in Brom. It was not yet the middle of the forenoon.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-07-void-roads/manuscript/chapter-50.md

# Chapter 50 — The Square of Grey

They had the day in that room by the colour of one small square.

It stood at the head of forty steps, a man's height on each side, and for the first hours after the lamp went out it was grey, and everything else was black. Cael learned the room by its sounds, and he went over it again and again, since it steadied him to know where each of them was.

Lira sat on the thirty-fourth step, six above the floor, with the bow along her knee and her eyes on the square, and Seln stood below her in the doorway at the stair's foot. Brom was on the ground against the wall beside that doorway, to the north of it, with three coats on him, and the healer sat on her heels at his left hand. Karis had gone to the wall opposite and put her back against it; and in the middle of the floor, where nobody went, a case lay in the dark with its cord.

That was six below, and there were six above; he had Seln's word for it every hour.

Seln went up for them himself: he climbed to the fifth step from the top and no higher, and lay along the stair with his chin on the stone, so that his eyes came level with the floor outside and nothing else of him did. From there, he said, the rim stood all round against the sky like the edge of a dish, and he counted what was on it. Then he came down, without a sound, and said it to the room in his mild voice.

"Six. Two at the gate, two south, one west, one north. None has left his place."

"What are they doing?" said Brom, the first time.

"The one at the gate with the bow is doing what you would do. He is finding his range."

They heard it for themselves before he had finished speaking.

It was a small hard tick on the roof over their heads, like a pebble dropped on a lid, and after it a long dry skittering that went away across the stone. Karis drew her breath in, and nobody spoke until it had stopped.

"That is five," said Seln. "He means to learn whether he can put one into that hole from where he stands. The stair runs down to the west, and he is to the east of it and a little above. He is the only one of the four who could."

"Can he?" said Lira, from the stair.

"I should say that it will take him some time."

It took him the better part of the forenoon, and he did not hurry. A tick came, and a skittering, and then nothing for as long as it takes to walk a quarter of a mile; and then another. Cael counted them, since he could not help it: some fell short and rang on the far side of the roof, and one struck so near the hole that they heard it skate past the lip.

"Six. Seven. Eight," said Seln, at intervals, from the doorway. He might have been reading out a floor schedule. "Nine. Ten. Eleven."

The square had gone from grey to white; it was near noon, and bright up there, and a little of the brightness came down as far as the tenth step and lay on it like spilt milk.

Then there was a sound that was not on the roof.

It was in the stair. It began high up, a crack and a rattle, and it came down toward them very fast, striking one edge and the next, like a stick drawn along a fence. Cael heard Lira's breath stop, and he heard the thing leap, and strike, and slide.

It stopped.

For the length of three breaths nobody moved. Then Lira spoke, and her voice was quite level.

"It's on the thirty-first step. I can see the feathers. That's three above my knee."

"Twelve," said Seln.

"He's got the hole," said Brom.

"He has the hole. He has not the stair. That bolt came in at the top and spent itself on thirty steps of stone; it would not have gone through a coat when it reached her." Seln had not raised his voice. "He knows that as well as I do. He wished us to know that he can reach the door. It is a kind of knock."

Cael had gone to the foot of the stair, where he could see her against the white, a dark shape with one arm, and the bow lying along her knee with its nose toward the square. It had not stirred.

"You didn't shoot," he said.

"At what? There's nobody in it." She did not turn her head. "I've one in the groove. If I loose it at a patch of sky I've an empty bow that I can't span, and Seln has to come up six steps into the light to do it for me. He'd like that, up there. He'd like to see me waste it."

"I only wondered."

"A man on a gate-walk told me that shooting's stopping. I've stopped. I'll go on being stopped till there's something standing in that square." Her right hand moved on the stock, a finger's breadth, and lay still. "It stays in the bow."

---

The white lasted a long time, and they filled it with small things, since there was nothing else to fill it with.

There was the water, of which they had five flasks among six, since the healer had come over a stone at a run with nothing in her hands; and Brom, who could not lift his right arm, had them all set down in front of him in the dark and shook each of them at his ear.

"Two swallows apiece at noon," he said. "And I'm keeping two full, shut, for the top. I'll not have us get out of this hole and find that we've nothing to walk on."

"You're very sure that we get out," said Lira, from the stair.

"I'm sure about the water. Somebody's got to be sure about something."

There was the bread, which was his own, and black on both faces. He had it broken into six by feel, and when the healer told him that he was to eat his share and not hide it in his coat for somebody else, he ate it, with a great deal of noise, so that she should hear him do it.

"I've a list," he said presently. He said it to the roof. "I've been keeping it since the ford. There was a dog that had my arm, and I drowned it. There was a cart that I had by the tail. A bowstring. Now there's a man on a hill whom I never saw." He shifted, and grunted. "It's been a better class of thing each time. I'd like that noticed."

"It's noticed," said Karis.

"What's next after a man on a hill?"

"I don't know," said Oryn. "But you'll sit still for it. You're grey, and you're no worse than you were, and I'd like to keep both of those."

She had two fingers on his wrist again. Cael had heard her do it a dozen times since the lamp went out: there was a small shift of cloth, and then her lips, counting to thirty, and then a word, which was always the same word, *there*. She could not read him; she could count his heart as any woman in a steading could, and she did it every quarter of an hour by some clock that she carried in her head.

"I learned a thing at the other one," she said, after one of those counts, to nobody. "At the stone. I learned how long an arm keeps when it's bound, and how far a person can be carried. It came to about two hundred yards." He heard her rub her palms together. "It's a little over two hundred from the head of that stair to where I'm a healer. I've been sitting here with that figure since the morning. It's a short walk. I never saw a longer."

The cold was the worst of it. There was no frost in that room, and no wind; but the stone drank the warmth out of whatever touched it, steadily, as dry ground drinks water, and Cael had given his coat to Brom, and by the middle of the white he could not feel his feet. He walked. He went along the south wall with his fingers on it, ten strides and back, and at the fourth turn his hand met wool.

"That is my shoulder," said Karis.

She was sitting with her back against the stone and her knees drawn up. He could not see her, but he could hear that she had her right hand raised, and that it was moving, very slowly, over the wall beside her head.

"You're reading it."

"I am touching it. That is a different thing, and I had better be honest about which." Her voice was small and exact in the dark. "It is cut as the bands on those stones were cut. I can find a hooked stroke with two bars across its stem under my second finger. I rubbed that stroke on the ninety-sixth afternoon, and dropped a grain into a cup for it."

"Then it's the same hand. The same sort."

"I think so. I shall not say so. I cannot tell by touch whether the bars stand close or wide, and it was the close and the wide that filled my bowl." She let her hand come down. "I have a wall here that I would give a year of my life to read. It is against my spine. My paper is in a box at a stone, and there is a finger of oil in that lamp, and the oil is for his shoulder if it turns. I have gone over that three times, to see whether I could make it come out otherwise. It will not."

"You could ask her for the lamp."

"I could. She would refuse me, and she would be right, and then both of us would have had to hear it said." She was silent for a breath. "I have a notebook and a pencil. I could draw what my finger finds. I should be drawing what I supposed, in the dark, and writing a date on it. I have struck that out of better people's work than mine."

He sat down beside her, since there was nothing that he could say to it. Her hand found his sleeve in the dark, and patted it twice, clumsily, as she might have patted a book that she meant to come back to.

---

It was Seln who said what the day was for, and he waited until the square had begun to lose its white.

He had come down from his count, and the six were where they had been. He stood in the doorway with what little light there was behind him, and spoke to the whole of the room.

"I have had since this morning to read it, and I am fairly sure of my reading. You should have it."

"Go on," said Cael.

"The bolt was for the last man. It was put into him five yards inside the stone, where a healer stood watching, by somebody who can hit a shoulder at forty-five yards and chose to. They could have had any of us, anywhere. They wanted a wound that would bring her over, and a fence to shut behind her." His mild voice did not alter. "A crew with a hurt man and a healer at the stone might have fought its way back to her. A crew with a hurt man and a healer who has no hands will sit still."

"That's what I said," said Oryn. "On the road. I said that it was for me."

"You did. I am only setting it in its place." He went on. "There are six of them, for a rim of sixteen hundred paces. That is a quarter of a mile to each station, and nobody to spare. It is why they have not come down. If one leaves his place, there is a gap in the dish that five people might run for. They will not come in, and they do not need to."

"Why don't they need to?" said Lira.

"Because there will be a frost tonight like the last. We have no fire. We have a man with iron in him, who will be a good deal worse by morning in this cold, and a healer who can do nothing for him until he is carried past a certain stone. They have only to stand where they are. Some time tomorrow we shall come up that stair with our hands open, to buy two hundred yards of grass."

It was a plain sum, and each of them had been doing a piece of it by himself in the dark; and now somebody had added it up aloud.

"With what?" said Brom at last. "What do we buy it with?"

Cael heard the small sound that a strap makes when a hand closes on it.

"I have thought about that too," said Seln. "I could walk up those steps now, with this held over my head. They would take it. I do not think that the fence would come down afterward. A paper that sends six people to a hole in the ground is not concerned with what becomes of anybody who could say how the thing was collected." He paused. "I may be wrong. I have been inside that service, and I do not think that I am."

"You're not going up," said Cael.

"No. I only wished it known that I had looked at it."

Cael got up from the wall, and his legs were stiff. He went across to the foot of the stair, to be nearer the light, and found that Lira had turned on her step to meet him, six above the floor, and was sitting with her chin on the stock of the bow.

"You're doing it again," she said.

She had pitched it for him alone.

"On that road, this morning. I watched you. Every one of them was up in you, and you put them all down. And you've sat in this hole since with fire in your hand that'd warm him, and you've not lit so much as a wick."

"Yes."

"I'm not saying that you're wrong. I've an arm that says you weren't wrong the last time." Her eyes were on the square. "I'm saying that I can see it. I'd not have you think that it went by me."

"There were six of them looking, on the road. Down here there's nobody; but a light shows at the top of a stair, and a warm man is a thing that somebody up there might wonder about tomorrow." He said it low. "Seln's rule is the only thing we have that they haven't. If I spend it to be comfortable, I've spent it."

"I know. It's a good rule." She was silent for a breath. "It's hard to watch, though. I thought that you ought to be told so by somebody who wasn't angry."

---

The square was going grey when he had it.

He had been at it since the morning, as he went at anything: a thing at a time, from the beginning, leaving nothing out. He did not have an answer; but he could say the whole of the question, and he had learned at a healer's table that this was most of the way.

"I want to read it out," he said. "The whole design. Stop me where I've got it wrong."

They turned toward him in the dark; he heard them do it.

"There's a paper, priced so that we'd come. There's a floor at the end of it where a Wind can't run, and an Iron Skin is a big man, and an Ember can't light her own lamp, and a Shadow has a knife and fifteen years. There's a bolt, placed to bring a healer over a line. There's a fence behind her. There are four bows on a rim, far enough back that we can't reach them and near enough to shoot anybody who climbs. And there's a night." He drew a breath. "Every piece of it does one thing. It takes away what one of us can do. It's a list with six names on it and a line through each."

"Five names," said Karis.

"No. That's the part I've been all day finding. It's got six. It thinks the floor did the same thing to all six of us."

He let that lie.

"So everything that's built on it is built for people who can do nothing. They watch the hole for somebody climbing out with his hands up. They've a fence that needs no guard, since nobody inside can so much as see it. Seln says they'll stand where they are till morning, and I think so too; they've no cause to move." He turned to the doorway. "What would they be watching for? If they thought that one of us might try something."

"A shout," said Seln at once. "Some declaration: a crack, a light. It is what anybody watches for in a practitioner. And on this ground it cannot come, so they are not watching for it very hard."

"Then that's what I can't do. I can't shout. If one of them sees a thing work on this side of that stone, there's a boat." He went through them aloud, since four of the people in that room knew them as well as he did. "The Wind cracks. The heavy one would lay the grass flat for thirty feet. Fire's fire; the Pressure can be seen gathering; the edge rings. That's five that shout. The read and the Compression are no use past the length of my arm, and the tenth does nothing at all in a fight."

"That's eight," said Brom. "You've ten."

"There's the one that I keep shut. It was made for not being seen. And there's the one that I had from the hand on the Ostrand road." He looked toward the place where the healer was. "I was inside a thing like that fence once, with nineteen points round me. I let it go from where it touched me, and the whole of it came down like a row of stitches. I've done that one time."

"Can you do it to this?" said Oryn.

"I don't know. I tried it a second time, on a doorway, with Lira on the far side, and I couldn't get hold. I could see it and I couldn't reach it; seeing isn't reach. I'd have to find where this one's made fast, and get my hand on that place, from the inside. I can't see any of it from farther than ten feet." He made himself finish. "And it's better than two hundred yards from here, with six of them looking down."

The room took that in for the space of several breaths.

"Then you go up alone," said Seln. "At dusk. With the one that you were given."

His voice was as mild as ever; and Cael, who had waited all through that reckoning for somebody else to say it, saw now that it could not have come from anybody else in the room, since it was the giver's to say what a gift was for.

"I shall say the condition, since it is to be used, and since I am the one here who was trained to count who is looking. That one was given, as I understand the matter, to be kept from any person who could carry the seeing of it west. Count who that is, today. It is six people, and every one of them is two hundred and sixty yards from that hole and can see nothing below its lip. If you take it on the stair, under the floor, there is no line from any of them to you; and from that step onward they have nothing to see, which is the whole of what the thing does." He folded his hands. "You let it go again where the ground hides you. Then what they have is a fence that failed, and a boy getting up out of a ditch."

"And after?" said Lira. "When he's out, and they've four bows?"

"After, he is to do everything that he has forbidden himself all day, as loudly as he can, on the far side of that stone. Their whole plan is that nobody on this ground has a Path. The instant that one of us stands outside the fence with something in his hands, and the fence is down behind him, there is no plan. Five more of us may walk out and be what we are." Seln's head turned toward Cael. "They were sent for a case. They were not sent to fight in the dark for it. I know what my own orders would have been, and so do they."

"It comes at dusk," said Cael. "It always has. I'll go when the square's nearly gone."

He unbuckled Hesk's satchel, with the book in it, and carried it up the stair and laid it on the stone beside Lira's boot.

"You've read it over my arm. If it wants reading again, it's yours first."

She put her right hand on it and said nothing.

The healer had got to her feet; he could hear it, and he knew where she had stopped: a stride and a half from him, in the pitch dark, as she stopped everywhere.

"Ask first," said Oryn.

"I'm not reading anyone."

"I know. I'm saying the rule out loud so it's been said on this floor too."

He understood her. She had no hands in that room, and no purse, and a man bleeding whom she could not mend; she had one thing that was hers, and she was setting it down on the stone where he would have to step over it.

"It's been said. I heard it."

He turned to the stair, and Lira's fingers had closed on his sleeve. He had a good deal that he wished to tell her, about a road that morning and an arm against a wall; and he had got as far as drawing breath for it when she shook her head, once, against the grey.

"Later."

He gave it back to her exactly as she had given it.

"Later."

---

He went up thirty steps on his own feet, like anybody.

At the fourth from the top he stopped. The square was over his head, and it was the colour of slate; he could see one star in it. Below him the stair went down into black, and he knew that a girl with one arm was looking up it at his back. He knew that Seln had turned round in the doorway and was facing the room, which was his way of saying that he had seen the thing before and did not need to see it again; and three more people were beyond the corner of that door, and could have seen nothing if they had tried.

Above him there was the lip of the floor, and a great deal of open sky. He was a yard below the level of the stone, and no person on that rim could have seen the top of his head.

It was dusk. Shadow, the ninth fragment that he carried, had been lifting in him for an hour, as it lifted at every dusk, like a tide coming up a beach. He had told it no on the fifth evening past the Line, and at a ford with the light going, and on a gate-walk; he had refused it on something over a hundred evenings, and let it have him once, for ten strides.

He did not tell it no.

The sound of his own breath stopped. That was how it came: the world went on exactly as before, and he went out of it. He lifted his boot to the third step and set it down, and no stone answered, and he could not hear his coat. Far below him he heard a small sharp breath that was Lira's, since she had been looking at a boy on a stair, and now she was looking at a stair.

He went up over the lip and stood on the floor.

He had made up his mind about the next thing in the dark. There were ten bolts lying on that grey somewhere, where they had skated and stopped, and one kick would send any of them ringing; so he gave the read the ground, which was the oldest and quietest thing that he owned, and it went out under his soles for its pace and a half, as it always did. It told him what was there, and did not tell him what anything meant. But a spent bolt has a weight, and he felt the first of them lying a yard ahead and a little to the left, like a pencil on a table, before his boot could find it.

He stepped over it.

It took the whole of his attention, and he saw that at once, and saw the price of it. He was a boy walking across thirty yards of bare stone in the last of the light, with six people looking at that stone; and he could not raise his eyes to them. He had them in his head only: two on the brow in front of him, two away on his left, one on his right, and one behind, where the west was still green. He walked with his eyes in the ground, and stepped over four bolts, and went round a fifth.

Then there was frozen grass under him, and the old road.

He took the southern of its two ruts, since that was the side that the newer bar stood on. It was a trough a foot deep and as wide as a wheel, with turf grown down into it, and it ran straight up the fall of the ground to a stone that he could not yet see; and he had a hundred and ninety yards of it to walk.

The cold began at fifty.

He had been waiting for it. A thing made for hiding costs from the moment that a person wakes; he had paid that on every day since it came to him, by carrying it shut. To have it open was a different bill. It began in the ends of his fingers, as it had begun in a lane on the Fallow, and it was not the frost, since it came from inside. At fifty strides he could not feel his nails; at a hundred his hands hung at the ends of his arms like things that he was carrying for somebody, and the cold had gone into his wrists.

He looked up once, at a hundred and twenty.

He could not have said why, and he paid for it at once. The brow stood against the eastern sky, dark, and on it there were two shapes. One was sitting, with something long laid across its knees; the other stood a little to the south of the first, with its hands drawn up inside its sleeves, as a person stands who has need of his fingers and means to keep them warm.

The read went out from under his feet. He had taken his mind off it for the length of one glance, and his boot came down on a loose stone in the rut and turned it over.

It made no sound, since nothing that he did made any sound. But he stood for ten breaths with his heart going, and his eyes in the ground again, and gave the read back to the turf before he took another step.

At a hundred and fifty there was a noise that nearly stopped that heart altogether.

It was the grey mule. She was hobbled above the stone with the brown one, and she had put up her head and brayed at the dusk, long and rusty, as she had no doubt brayed at every dusk of her life. The brown mule did not bray. She had turned her ears, both together, down the old road, and she was looking straight at the place where he stood.

On the brow, the sitting shape turned its hood toward the two beasts.

It looked at them for as long as it takes to count five, and saw a mule that was making a noise and a mule that was not, and three boxes under a blanket. Then the hood came round again to the floor, and to the square hole in the middle of it, where nothing had stirred all day.

Cael walked on, and he could not feel his arms below the elbow.

---

At fourteen feet from the newer bar there was nothing.

He had known that there would not be. He could see the iron itself, a dark stroke above the frosted turf, and the grey stone beyond it; and the air between that bar and its fellow was as empty as any air. He took a stride, and another, and let Anchor look.

At twelve feet there was the greyness, like breath on glass. At ten, they were there.

There were three courses across the old road, at the knee and the chest and over the head, exactly as he had seen them thrown in the morning; they were as thick as his wrist, and they had not slackened in a day. He went along them with that other sight to the newer bar, where he had watched them arrive, and looked for the place where they were made fast.

It was not there.

He stood in a ditch with the cold in his elbows and looked again. The three courses came to the newer bar and touched it, and there was a point where each of them met the iron; but they did not end. They went through those points as a rope goes through a ring, and on, southward along the edge, out of his sight.

He had got it wrong. He had watched three lines fly across a road toward a bar, and had taken the bar for the end of them. A rope is not made fast where it lands; it is made fast where the hauling starts.

He turned his head, slowly, to the north.

The old bar stood eight feet away across the grown-over road, under its five cold terms of rust. He was eleven feet from it, perhaps, where he stood, and at that distance he could make out only that there was something on it. He climbed out of the southern rut, and crossed the crown of the old road on his hands and knees, since the turf there stood a foot higher and he did not trust the thing that hid him to hide him against the sky; and he let himself down into the northern rut.

It was there.

It was on the far side of the iron, the side toward the brow, a hand above the turf, and he saw it through the bar, as a person sees a lamp through his own fingers. It was a single point, as big as a fist. Three courses came into it from the south, across the road; and three came into it from the north, round the whole of that great circle, the other end of the same rope. Nothing came out. Everything that held six people in a low place on the hundred and fourteenth day was hauled taut through that one point and belayed there, like the whole of a ship's rigging brought down to a single pin.

He could see it. He had been able to see a line across a doorway once, with a girl's shoulder pressed against it, and it had done him no good whatever.

He lay down in the rut on his left side, with the bar at the length of his arm. He put his right hand out toward it, and round it, and could not tell when he had touched it; he had to watch his own fingers close on the scale to learn that they had. Past the iron, his hand was on the far side of that edge, and he laid the dead palm against the outer face, low down.

It was against him now, as the first had been against his coat; and he was inside what it held.

*A thing set against a surface and told to stay.*

He told it not to.

It went as a breath goes that a person has been holding, and the point was simply not under his palm. The three courses to the south dropped across the old road, and he saw the nearest ten feet of them go, like wet rope falling; and from the north, away round the dark edge of the low ground, he felt the rest come in and go slack and run out through the place where a knot had been, a great deal of it, for as long as it takes to draw a bucket. He had not known how much ground it held until it stopped holding.

Forty paces above him, on the brow, a standing shape flung both its arms forward out of its sleeves, as a man does when a rope is dragged through his hands.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-07-void-roads/manuscript/chapter-51.md

# Chapter 51 — Declaring

He let Shadow go while he lay in the ditch, with his arm still round a bar of the registry's iron.

It went out of him as it had come. The sound of his own breath returned, loud and ragged, and after it the creak of frozen grass under his hip and the grey mule shifting her feet above the stone. He was a boy lying in an old rut at dusk, under the turf's lip, where nobody on that brow could have seen more of him than a shoulder; and whatever they had looked at for the last quarter of an hour, it had not been him.

He drew his arm back. He could not feel it. Both his hands were dead to the elbow, with a cold that had nothing to do with the frost, and when he pushed himself up on them it was like leaning on two posts.

He stood.

The six on the rim were where they had been all day: two on the brow above him, two away to the south, one north, and one far off across the low ground against the last of the light. The two on the brow were on their feet; the one with the bow had it up across his chest, spanned, and the other was holding his own hands out before him and looking into them.

Cael stepped out of the rut and walked across the edge, between the old bar and the stone.

He did it slowly, since he wished it seen. He set his boots down on the outside of a line that five people could not cross that morning, and turned his face up the old road to the brow, and stood there with his arms hanging. It was forty paces. In that light he was a dark upright thing against paler grass, and the man above him had hit a shoulder at five paces more.

The bow came round.

He had priced this on the stair. There was one bolt in that groove, and when it was gone, a boot must go into a stirrup and a hook onto a string, and a back must straighten; he had watched that man do it once, from a frozen road, and it had taken him something over twenty counts. One bolt, then, and after it twenty counts in which a bow was a stick. He had been told on a bluff never to spend the Wind near a thing whose single blow he could not price. He could price this one to a hair.

He set his feet as Lira had taught him to set them.

*Front. Back. Shoulders.* He loaded nothing on the third. He stood through it, with Wind lying quiet in his soles, and he watched a hooded shape forty paces up a hill. A girl had come down off a gate-walk eight days ago with a sore palm and three words, which a lean man had given her. Shooting was stopping. The stock on the brow settled into a shoulder. The shape behind it grew smaller, as a person does who is letting out his breath. And then it was entirely still.

*Four, and.*

The air cracked like a whip over the stone, and the frost went up off the grass in a white fan. He was twenty feet to the north, in pale stems to the knee, and something had gone through the place where he had been with a sound like cloth tearing. He heard it strike the frozen road, a long way down behind him.

The landing locked.

*One.* Everything that he owned went dumb. For the length of that count there was a boy standing on a hillside who could not have shifted a foot to save his life; and he spent it looking up at the brow, since his eyes were the only part of him that would answer. The man with the bow had not stood to watch his bolt. He had the nose of the thing on the turf already, and his boot going into the stirrup.

Then the count was done, and Cael ran.

He went up the slope on a slant, toward the old road, with the frozen grass breaking under him and forty paces of hill before him, and his hands swinging at the ends of his arms like things that he had borrowed. He counted as he ran, and it was the bowman's count that he kept and not his own: at seven the hook was on the string, and at eleven the back was beginning to come up.

At twelve yards short of the brow he reached the lip of the old rut, and he gave up the ground.

It was like shutting an eye. The pace and a half that had been under his soles since a stair went out from under them. He let Storm, the heaviest thing that he owned, have the air instead, and he laid the lane up the rut.

It was a corridor as long as a cart and team, set down in that trough with its high end at his feet and its low end up the hill, and everything in it went one way at once. Frost went, and the seed that hung heavy on the grass at either lip, and chaff, and the dust of a hundred dry days; it went up that channel in a grey flood and burst out over the brow, twelve yards on, into two hoods.

He let it go, and began to pay.

---

For ten counts after a lane, everything that he had came to him a beat late.

He had run up a ditch on the Fallow in that lag, with hounds behind him, and he knew it; but he had not known what it was to close with a man in it. His boots struck the turf, and then he was told that they had struck. The brow came up to meet him. There was a hooded shape in a cloud of chaff with one arm across its face, and it had let the string go half drawn and caught the bow up by its stirrup end.
