He saw the stock begin to move. Then he saw where it was, which was a good deal nearer.

It was coming round at his head like a flail. He got his right arm up. It was a dead arm, and it was late, and for the first beat nothing in him answered at all; a pound of ash wood and horn met the bone of his forearm with the whole of a strong man's swing behind it. His knees began to go.

Then Compression, which a fighter had put through him on a circuit, long ago, arrived and did what it was for.

There was only one road open to it. The blow went down the bone into his shoulder, and down his spine, and through both his heels into the hill; and the hill took it. The stock stopped against his arm as it would have stopped against a gatepost. He felt it ring from the wrist to the elbow, and he knew that he would be paying for that for a week.

The hooded man had both his hands stung off the bow. It fell between them.

Cael did not strike him. He could have; the man stood open, a yard off, with his cloth blown half across his hood and grass-seed in the folds of it, and there was nothing in that hood that Cael could have sworn to afterward but that it was the height of a person. He looked at it for the length of one late breath, and turned away from it, to the south.

The other stood two yards off.

He had not run, and he had not reached for anything, since he carried nothing to reach for. He stood with his feet apart on the trodden ruts, and both hands held out before his chest, bare, with the palms turned toward Cael and the fingers a little spread. They were quite still.

Cael knew that stillness. He had knelt in a chalk cut with nineteen points round him, and had watched a woman make it fifteen feet away, with the whole of her attention gone down into the ends of her fingers; it was the stillness of threading a needle. In another breath there would be something set against the front of his coat, and told to stay.

He did not let Anchor look. He had no need to; he had the hands.

They were a man's hands, broad, and short in the finger. That was all that he was ever to know of their owner. There was a pale band round the third finger of the left, where a ring had been taken off before the work began.

His own left hand had found his knife. He could not feel the hilt. He drew it, and held it out at the full length of his arm, with the point a hand's breadth under those two open palms; and he asked Ember for the one thing that it had.

A point of fire stood on the knife's tip.

It was no bigger than a candle's. It lit the palms from beneath, and the grey cuffs, and nothing above them. He held it there. He said no word, and he did not move the blade nearer by the width of a hair.

A person cannot thread a needle with a flame under his hands.

He saw the fingers want to. He saw them hold their stillness for one breath, and for a second, with the heat coming up into them. Then they closed, slowly, and the hands drew back, and went down to the man's sides.

The man took one step backward. He took another. He did not turn his hood away from the flame until he was five yards off; and Cael did not follow him by a single stride.

It was the bowman who ended it. He had got the chaff out of his cloth, and he bent, without haste, and took up the bow from the turf. He did something to the lock of it with his thumb, and a small dark thing like a walnut came away into his palm and went into his coat. He drew a short knife across the string, and then he laid the bow down again in the ruts, carefully, on its side, as a workman lays down a tool that was only lent him.

The two of them went north along the carters' road, side by side, at a trot. They did not run until they were sixty yards off.

Cael turned to the low ground, and lifted the knife over his head.

The lag had gone out of him. He stood on the brow in the last of the dusk, with a point of fire at the end of his arm where anybody on that rim could see it, and he looked for the other four.

To the south there were two shapes still. One of them, the one that had stood all day with its arms folded, raised a single arm above its hood and let it fall. Then both stooped, and straightened, and went away over the rim to the south, with nothing in their hands.

When he looked to the north, there was nobody there at all.

The west was the last. The sky there was green yet, low down, and the rim stood against it as black as a cut edge. A small upright shape had stood on that edge since first light and had never once loosed. It bent now, and laid something down. It walked away; and at the top of the rim, where another step would have taken it out of his sight, it stopped, and turned itself round, and looked back across five hundred yards of grass at a boy with a light.

He saw the hood against the sky. There was the shape of it, and the shoulders under it, and inside it there was nothing whatever. Then that was gone too.

Six. He counted them off that rim as Seln had counted them onto it. He let the fire go out, and walked down forty paces to the grey stone, and called one word down the old road into the dark.

"Back."

---

They came up out of the low ground in file, as they had gone down.

He heard them long before he saw them. There was no need for quiet now. Brom's breathing came first, heavy and careful, and then a shuffle of boots on frozen turf; and at last five shapes grew out of the dusk on the old road, with a small one in front that made no sound at all.

Seln stepped over the stone, and stood a breath on the outside of it. Cael saw his shoulders come down by the width of a finger, as a man's do when a Path that he has done without all day is handed back to him.

Karis was next, and she had said that she would never grow used to it. She crossed, and stopped, and put out her right hand in front of her in the dark. A point of light stood on the air above her palm, as small and steady as a star.

"I have a hand," she said, to nobody. Her voice shook. "I have a hand again."

She set the point on the wick of her lamp.

The healer came over between two of them, with Brom's good arm across her shoulders and his weight on her; and Cael watched her do what he had seen her do at a rock on the Fallow, eight times in one grey morning, when she thought that nobody saw. She took one stride past the stone. She stopped dead, with her eyes shut and her lips moving, and her free hand closed on Brom's wrist.

The count went to four, and on, and did not stop.

"There. Back," she said. It came out of her on one breath, hoarse, as a person speaks who has been holding it since the morning. She opened her eyes and looked at her own left hand in the lamplight, back and palm. "The clock started again."

Then she was a healer.

"Down. Here, on the blanket, on your left side. You, bring that light and hold it where I point. You, the pannier, the one that's on the grey." She had Brom on the turf before any of them had moved. "And somebody give me a knife that'll cut ash."

It was Seln's. She had him kneel behind Brom's shoulder and cut through the shaft below the iron head, a notch at a time, while she held the wood still in both her fists so that it should not turn. It took as long as it takes to say a line over twice; then the head came away, and she threw it on the grass, and put one hand flat on the front of the shoulder and took hold of the feathered end with the other.

"Breathe out," she said. "All of it. I'll not lie to you; you'll know that it's leaving."

She drew the shaft back out the way that it had come, in one pull.

Brom made no sound that could be heard at ten yards. His boots moved on the turf. Her two hands were round the shoulder before the wood was clear, one above the place and one below; her eyes shut; and Cael, who had sat all day in a stone room and heard her count that man's heart with two fingers, saw her lips reach three and stop counting, since she had what she wanted.

It was an hour, by the stars. Lira held the lamp for all of it with her one hand, sitting on the grey stone, and did not once let the light shake.

"That's one," said Oryn at the end of it. She sat back on her heels. Her face was the colour of the frost. "It's shut. It went through the meat and missed everything that matters, and you were born lucky. You'll carry nothing on that side till I've said so."

"I've another side," said Brom.

"I've seen it. Be quiet." She turned on her knees. "You. Give me that arm, and give me the lamp to somebody else."

Lira gave the lamp to Karis. She held out the sling without a word, and the healer undid her own knot, and unwound a strip of Cael's shirt that had gone stiff and dark, and put her two hands round the forearm.

It took a quarter of an hour. When she had finished she did not let go of the wrist at once.

"It held a stair. It's not going to hold anything else this season."

"It did hold it," said Lira. Her voice was small. "I'd have gone down the other twenty on my head."

"I know that it held. I felt where. I'm not scolding the arm." Oryn drew the wool up and tied it, shorter than before, so that the hand lay high against the collar-bone. "That's two, on a road, after three at a bench yesterday. There isn't a third in me tonight for anybody, and I'd thank you all to stay whole till morning."

---

He paid the rest of his own bill sitting against the old iron, with his knees drawn up.

It came all at once, as it had come at the stone on the Fallow. His right forearm was dead from the wrist to the elbow, where a stock had been stopped, and across his left palm there was a white weal as long as a finger. He had held a knife with a flame on its point in a hand too cold to know that the hilt was hot, and he had found it out only when he tried to open that hand again. He could not stop shaking. He had had a week of barley sacks and tables to rest in, and he had spent the whole of what it gave him between a stair and a brow, in less time than a kettle takes.

The healer looked at the palm from her stride and a half.

"I can't touch that. You know why." She threw him what was left of Lira's tape. "Wind it round, and keep it out of the frost. It'll close faster than it ought to. Yours always do."

Seln had gone to the mules.

He went round the three boxes under their blanket with the lamp held low, and stooped over each cord in turn, and touched nothing. When he came back his face was as it had been all day.

"I tied those to be read. There is a stem of grass laid under every knot, and each is where I laid it." He set the lamp down. "Nobody has had a hand on them. I had thought that it would be so. Six people with a quarter of a mile of rim apiece do not leave it to look into a mule's load."

"What did they see?" said Cael. "Tell me as you'd write it for them."

"I have been composing it since you called." Seln folded his hands. "A fence was laid round that ground before light, and shut behind six people after sunrise. It held all day. At dusk it failed, at its knot, which was set on an iron bar that stands upon the very edge of the place. Directly afterward a boy stood up out of the old road beside that bar and walked across the edge. He declared a Wind, and something heavy in a ditch, and a fire, and he took a blow that ought to have broken his arm."

"And between the stair and the bar?"

"Nobody on that rim saw anything between the stair and the bar. I asked Lira what she saw from the thirty-fourth step. She says that she saw a stair." His mild eyes rested on Cael. "They will write that he crept up a rut in bad light while they were watching a hole. They will write that a knot tied a hand's breadth from such an edge is a knot that nobody should have trusted. A man who lays fences for his living would far sooner put that on paper than the other thing, and he has no cause to think of the other thing at all. It holds."

He turned, and went up the old road to the brow by himself.

When he came down he had a crossbow in his hands.

He brought it into the lamplight and turned it over, slowly, as he turned over any paper. It was a good bow, heavier than the healer's, with a prod of horn and sinew. The string hung from one end of it, cut, and the lock was an empty socket. He ran his thumb along the underside of the stock, and under the stirrup, and across the butt.

"There was a maker's stamp here. It has been taken off with a file, and the place oiled. There is no armoury number. It was bought for one day, by somebody who meant from the first to leave it lying." His voice had not risen. Cael had never heard it so quiet. "I walked past the place where that man lay, twice. I have sat in a hole since sunrise with his bolt on the stair. I have his bow. And I could not tell you tonight whether he was tall."

He took the thing by its two ends.

Cael had known him for two years. He had seen him carve a goat, and wax a seal, and stand in a doorway with his back to a room, and he had never seen him do a thing in anger. Seln lifted the crossbow over his head and brought it down across the grey stone with the whole of his narrow strength, and the stock broke in two at the lock. He stood over it, breathing.

"No faces."

He laid the two pieces on the turf, side by side, and went and sat down by the mules.

Cael opened Hesk's book on his knee. Lira had put it into his lap when she came over the stone, with the strap done up, and had said nothing; and he held the pencil in his bandaged left hand, since the right would not close.

*Hundred and fourteenth day, night. Outside the stone. All six out. Brom: a bolt through the right shoulder, drawn and mended, an hour. Lira: the arm opened on the stair wall and is shut again, a quarter. Thirteen bolts loosed at us in the day, and one hit. Ours: five, and none shot.*

He ruled a line, and put down what he had spent, one thing to a line, as he had on the Fallow.

*Anchor: looking, at the two bars, first light. I saw the fence go up. I stood two counts to see it, and Brom had those two counts with a bolt in him.*

*Shadow: once, from the fourth step to the old bar. Lira saw it go, from below. Seln turned his back. Three were round the door. The six were two hundred and sixty yards off, with a lip between; not one had a line to me. Let go in the rut. It holds. Dead to the elbows; I had to watch my own hand.*

*The read: the ground, from the hole to the bar. All my eyes. I looked up once, and it left me.*

*Anchor: looking, for the knot. I can't find what that costs. Entered as not found.*

*Anchor: the unbinding. Hand on it, from inside. Once.*

*Wind: one burst, off the stone. The lock.*

*Storm: one lane, up the old rut. Ten counts late.*

*Compression: in those ten. The right arm.*

*Ember: one point, on the knife. The left palm.*

He counted them with the pencil's end, and there were nine.

*Nine. With the reading on the road, ten since Thornwater. Deployment since the Line: twenty-three.*

He looked at that for a little. Then he wrote the rest beneath, as he had it.

*Second nameless team. Same hand. I own a piece of the first one and I used it on the second. The account's still unpayable. It's getting longer.*

---

The light came down the old road when it was full dark.

It was a lantern, and it was carried high, at the length of somebody's arm, as a person carries one who wishes it understood from a long way off that he is not creeping. Seln was on his feet before Cael had seen it. Lira had the bow across her knee.

"Four," said Seln, presently. "One with a spear. One who favours a leg."

They came over the brow in file, a crew's length apart, and the man in front stopped where the ruts turned, beside a thing that lay on the turf there. He held the lantern over it. Then he came on down, alone, and the three behind him stood where they were.

He was of the middle height, and heavy in the forearm, and there was a band of black leather laced on his right wrist. He stopped at the edge of their lamplight, and Cael saw his eyes go round it once, slowly. They went to a big man lying on his left side under a healer's coat, and to a girl whose sling was tied a hand higher than it had been at a certain door. They went to two pieces of a crossbow lying side by side by a grey stone. They came to Cael, and to his hands.

"You're late back," said Teague.

"We were kept."

"I saw some of it. We were on the ridge when the light went." He set the lantern down on the turf between them. "I'll say what I saw, so that you've no need to wonder what I'm carrying. There's a line down there. I've walked round it for years, and I know where it is by the ruts. I saw a man come over it from the inside."

He paused. He was choosing the next thing as he chose everything, by weight.

"You walked out of the Quiet declaring," he said.

"Yes."

Teague's eyes did not leave him, and Cael waited for the rest of it. There was a great deal that a captain might fairly have asked of somebody who had just broken a rim of six with nothing in his hands: he might have asked how, and with what, and what a boy was who had a Wind's crack in him and a fire as well. Cael watched him weigh each of those, and set every one of them down again, as he had set down a question at an inn's door two nights before, since he had no means to price the answer.

"Then you're not on the board anymore. You're something the board doesn't have a column for."

It was the second time in three days that Cael had been told so. A one-armed man had said it of a line in a book. This man said it of him, to his face, across a lantern, with no heat in it and no fear; he might have been reading out the weight of a load.

"I've three with me, and a light," said Teague. "They'll want that rim walked before any of you sleeps. I'll have it walked."

He took up his lantern, and went back up the old road to where his three were standing.

---

It had been the hundred and thirteenth morning, a day and a half before, when Teague took a slip that he did not want.

He had been at the plank before the mist was off the river. The five had gone by it in the dark, two hours earlier, and he had heard the mule. Pike was setting out his book; and there was a shingle on the oak, six along from the left-hand post, which had hung there for nine days because no crew of any standing would trouble with it.

*Millrace. Three stakes of the weir, gone in the last high water. To be set before the ground shuts. Four marks.*

Teague drew its tack and held it out.

Pike looked at the shingle, and at him, and at the shingle again.

"That's a boy's job."

"It's on my road."

"You've no road south. You've forty marks of fence east, with my capitals on it, and you've a day's posts still to set."

"Then it'll want its capitals a day or two longer."

Pike said nothing more. He wrote the name. But he kept his one hand on the shingle for a breath after the ink was dry, and Teague knew that he had been read; and he found, somewhat to his surprise, that he did not mind it.

He had told a boy at a door that his crew would be at Millrace in two days, and it had been true when he said it only because he had decided, between one sentence and the next, to make it true. He had been turning that over ever since. He had read a certain paper on a nail, and had hung it back, for the same reason that he had laid its fellow under a stone: there was something wrong in it that he could not put his hand on. He could not have told another captain what. A man does not walk up to a crew and hand it the hair on his own neck.

But he could be half a day from where the paper pointed, with a spear and a bow and a boy, on honest business. Nobody could say that it rested on nothing, since it rested on three stakes and four marks.

They walked at noon, and slept at the fork above the mill, without a fire, a crew's length apart; and before he lay down he counted his three by their breathing, as he did every night of his life. The bow's was the short one, since that hip had not liked the ridge.

It was the boy who asked, at that fork, before the blankets were down. He had a heel of bread half way to his mouth, and he stopped it there, as he stopped eating whenever his captain was about to speak.

"Is it the five? That we're up here for?"

"It's a weir. You heard the slip said."

"I heard it. I never knew you take four marks."

Teague looked at him for some time. He had made it a rule, nine cold terms ago, that he would not walk three people up a road for a reason that he was ashamed to give them; and he found that the rule held on a ridge at night as it held at a plank.

"There's a paper that I hung back on a nail," he said. "There's a crew that took it down. I think that it's a bad paper, and I can't tell you what's bad in it. I said to them that if they were late, we'd come and look."

"Look for what?" said the bow. He was easing his leg out along the turf, a little at a time.

"I'll know when I see it."

"That's not a price."

"No," said Teague. "It isn't. I've none to give you. If any one of you would sooner set stakes tomorrow and go home, he can say so now, and I'll think no worse of him."

The spear said nothing, which was what he said to most things. The bow grunted, and went on easing his leg. The boy put the bread into his mouth.

They were at the weir by first light on the hundred and fourteenth, and the woman who kept the ford told him the news before he had asked for it. Five had come off the ridge the day before, at noon, with a mule; and the healer had gone up again with them, and had taken the mill's jenny, and had left word with a carter for a child at some gate to the north.

"She's not one to leave her bench for a walk," said the woman. "Thirty rounds I've counted her through this ford. She never once went up that ridge."

He set two stakes with the spear and the boy, in water to the thigh, while the bow sat on the bank and sharpened the third. He did the sum as he drove them: it was an afternoon up to that place, by the ruts, and a morning to go in and bring a case out, if it was a walk and a carry, so that they would be down at the ford again by the middle of the day.

The middle of the day came. He stood in the mill water with a maul in his hands and looked up at the notch in the ridge, and nobody came through it.

He had not said how late. He saw that now. He had said *if you're late back*, as though late were a thing that would announce itself. He gave it one hour more by the shadow of the wheel.

Then he laid the maul down on the bank, beside the third stake.

"That's not set," said the boy.

"No. It'll cost us the four marks; she'll not put her mark to two stakes of three." He was wringing out his breeches. "Get your bread. We're going up."

Not one of the three asked him why. The spear took his spear. The bow got up off the bank, on the good leg first, and slung his stave; and Teague, who had been about to tell him that he might stay with the mill and the mules, saw his face and held his tongue.

---

They were on the ridge at dusk, on that same day, half a mile short of where the road tips over, when he saw it.

There was a light first. It was small, and low down, where the dark ground was; a point of fire that did not flicker as a brand flickers. Before he had fairly taken it in, there was a sound that he knew as well as his own name, since he had walked eleven cold terms beside a Wind, long ago, in another crew. That crack was a Wind's.

He put up his hand, and his three stopped behind him.

Then there was a noise in the low ground that he did not know at all. It was like a squall caught in a ditch. Something pale went up the slope in a column, where the old road ran, and burst.

And then the light again, in the same place. It was held still. It was held up, by one arm, as high as that arm would go.

He stood on the ridge with the wind in his face and counted what he had. A Wind was one thing. An Ember, who could make fire stand on the air, was another, and he had worked beside two such; and whatever had gone up that ditch was a third. He had seen all three within the space of forty breaths, at one spot, and when he looked for the people who had done them he could find only one upright shape, very small, with its arm raised.

He was too old to tell himself that he had miscounted.

There were others moving. He saw them along the far edge of the low ground, against what was left of the sky: two together, going away south, and one to the west. They did not go as beaten men go; they went as people go who have practised leaving. He had seen a crew break like that only once, and he had not liked that crew.

"We'll not run in on it," he said. "Give them the time to be gone. Then we light the lantern, and we carry it where it shows."

He read the ground by that lantern as they came down, since it was the thing that he could do.

On the brow, where the ruts turned, two people had lain all day, and the frost showed him the two shapes, two yards apart, each a man's length. There was no ash of any fire, and nobody had dropped a crumb there, or a thread, or the paring of a nail. Beside the northern shape he found the round print of a stirrup pressed into the turf again and again in the one place. He counted, and there were eleven prints, and a twelfth half made.

That was a crossbow. He had expected a bow of some kind. He had not expected that every sign of its owner would stop at the height of a boot.

He said his piece to the boy at the stone, and it was a short piece. He had said that he saw a man walk out of the Quiet and declare, which was true. He had not said three, since he could not price three, and he had put no question about what else there was. A captain who asks a question that he has no column for has only given himself something to carry, and he had three to carry already.

The healer had a big man under her hands, and kept her eyes on him.

He sent the spear south-about by the ruts with the lantern, and the boy with him. They were gone the better part of two hours. They came back by the north, having walked the whole of the ruts, with a crossbow each and a third across the boy's shoulders.

"Lying down," said the spear. He was not a man of many words. "On their sides. String cut. Nothing in the lock."

"And nothing else?"

"Nothing. I looked."

The bow had the three of them across his knee before they were fairly on the grass. He turned each to the lantern, and tried each empty lock with his thumb, and shook his head over them like a man at a horse fair.

"Three makers, and every one's had his name filed off. I never saw the like." He looked up. "They're a deal better than mine."

Teague did not answer him. He was looking down the old road, to where a lamp burned beside a grey stone, and a boy sat against a bar of iron with a book on his knee.

He had a rank that he had never sent west. He had earned it on a ledge in the cold, where there was no station to see it, and had let the registry go on writing the old one; it had seemed to him then that what a man had done was worth more than what was put down about him. So he knew a little about being something that a book had no line for. It was a very little. He had kept back one tier. That boy had no tier to keep back. Whatever he was, it was not written anywhere at all.

It made Teague uneasy, and he did not pretend otherwise. But six people had lain on this rim with their faces covered and their bows unmarked, and had shot a man from behind; and one boy had stood up in front of them with a light held where everybody could see it. He knew which of those two things he would sooner have on a road beside his three.

He did not sleep much. A while before the light he got up and woke them.

Nobody had asked it of him. The boy at the stone had asked him for nothing, either at an inn's door or across a lantern; he had only said *noted*, and *yes*. But a crew that has been shot at from four sides through the whole of a day will keep looking up for a month afterward, and Teague had seen what that does to people. If there was a thing to be fetched out of that hole, they would fetch it better without having to watch the sky.

He put the spear on the southern rim, where two had stood, and sent the boy west, since the boy had the legs for it. The bow he set on the north, which was the shortest walk, and he took the brow himself.

When the sun came up over the ridge behind him, there were four on the rim, and every one had his face bare.

---

## CHAPTER — editions/monroe-1.3/book-07-void-roads/manuscript/chapter-52.md

# Chapter 52 — Bearing

Cael went up to the brow when the sun was a hand above the ridge, and the man who stood there did not turn round until he was within five paces.

It was the hundred and fifteenth morning. He had slept as a stone sleeps, and woken to find that the ground came to his feet when it should, and that his right arm would bend. His left palm was wound in what remained of a roll of tape. He had counted four upright shapes on the rim before he had his boots on, one at each of the places where a hood had stood the day before; and for the length of a breath he had been on his feet with nothing in his hands.

Then he had seen that every one of the four had a face.

"You've put your crew round it," he said.

"I have." Teague was looking down at the floor, and at the square in the middle of it, with his thumbs in his belt, over the hand-axe. "There's a thing down there that you were sent for, and I'd guess that you mean to go and get it."

"Yes."

"Then you'll be an hour or two with your backs to the sky. I'd not care to be, after yesterday." His grey eyes came round. "We'll hold it till you're up."

Nobody had asked it of him. Cael had lain against a bar of iron the night before, shaking, and had watched a lantern go away round a quarter of a mile of ruts to look for whatever six people had left; he had thought then that it was more than a captain owed to a crew ten lines under him, and he had not known how to say so. He did not know now. He had one word for a thing that he could neither refuse nor repay, and he had already given it to this man once.

"Noted," he said.

Teague nodded, by the width of a finger. Then he stooped, and took up the horn lantern that stood by his boot, and held it out.

"That's a deep hole. You'll want two lights."

Karis had the long cord out by the time he came down.

It was the same that she had made at Thornwater, two box-ropes spliced end to end and marked off with twists of red wool; and she had it laid along the turf from the grey stone northward, with its near end under her boot. She looked as though she had not slept, and she looked very happy.

"I said that I should pace it in the morning, and the morning was otherwise engaged." She was winding the slack round her fist. "Brom may not haul; he is to stand and be seen. So I want Seln for the turfs, and I want Lira on the line."

"I've one arm," said Lira.

"You have one arm, and a hand at the end of it, and a sigil, and I need exactly those three. You take the far end, and walk out along the edge with your boot half on it, until the cord is taut. Where you stop, he cuts."

"How do I walk along a thing that I can't see?"

"As you found it last night: your Arbiter will tell you which foot has gone over."

It took Lira the first three cords to learn the trick of it, and after that she did not put a foot wrong. Cael walked behind with the notebook, and wrote each station in a bad hand. She went with the cord's end shut in her right fist and the sling tied high against her collar-bone, a pace at a time, as a child walks along a kerb; and every few strides she tipped her head, as if she were listening, and moved her left boot a hand's breadth out or in.

"Gone," she would say, under her breath. "Back. Gone. It's like a wall that you can lean your foot on."

At each cord's end Seln knelt and cut a turf, and Karis came up along the rope, counting, and laid her short cord from the turf to the place where the long one would next lie; and at every station it was the same short measure, as it had been on the Fallow.

They went the whole way round, with the Quieting on their right hand and four people watching from the rim.

The spear stood on the south, a tall man, grounded, with the butt of his weapon on the frozen turf and both hands on the shaft; and he watched a girl with one arm find an edge with her foot, sixty times over, and said nothing whatever. On the west it was the boy, sitting on his heels in the ruts with a heel of bread. He stopped chewing when Lira came abreast of him, and he had not started again when they were a hundred yards on.

They passed a good many bars of iron. Every one was old, and scaled, and stamped with the same two figures, and each stood within a boot of a turf that Seln had just cut.

It was the middle of the forenoon when Lira's fist came back to the grey stone.

Karis went down the page with her pencil, twice. Then she turned back in the notebook to a leaf that was nineteen days old, and laid her finger on the foot of it, and looked from the one to the other.

"It is the same," she said. "Cord for cord, and the odd strides are the same number of strides." Her voice was not quite steady. "I said two hundred, from the ridge, by my eye, and I was twenty short of it, and a stride. It is the Fallow's round, and so it is the Fallow's radius."

"The same size," said Lira. "The both of them."

"To a stride. I should like somebody to tell me how a thing comes to be the same size twice, five days' walk apart, by accident." She shut the book on her finger. "Nobody will. I am going down to look at a wall."

---

They went down with two lamps, and the stair was a different place. Karis had filled her brass one to the neck from the can in the left-hand box.

The day before, Cael had gone down it at a run, in the dark, counting edges. He walked it now behind Teague's lantern. The steps were the grey of the floor, cut out of it and not laid; every edge was as sharp as on the day that it was made, since no foot had ever worn one. On the thirty-first a bolt lay, with three grey feathers, and Lira bent and took it up as she passed, and put it through her belt.

"That's twelve," she said. "He'll want it for his count."

At the twentieth the air changed, as it had changed in the dark, and both flames stood up straight and still. It was colder there than the frost had been, and there was no smell in it of earth, or of water, or of anything that grows.

They were all six of them on that stair. The healer came last.

She had said at the stone that she would not sit outside a second day and listen. Four people were holding the rim who knew what a crossbow was; and she had found out what it was worth to be on the right side of a line when the line was shut. She meant to be where a certain shoulder was; and if anything opened, it could be carried up forty steps and two hundred yards with her walking beside it.

Brom went in front of her, with his right arm bound across his chest, and did not once put his hand to the wall.

Cael catalogued the room properly, since nobody had been able to the day before.

It was ten strides from the door to the western wall, and ten across, and he paced both. The roof was the underside of the floor that they had crossed, a foot above Brom's head, without a beam or a crack; he held the lantern up to it and found his own light looking back at him, dully, as from still water. The floor was the same, and there was dust on it. It lay as thin as the bloom on a plum, and it had taken their boots of yesterday along the northern wall and beside the door, and nobody's anywhere else.

The walls were what he had seen by one small lamp, and a great deal more.

They were cut from the floor to the roof. The work ran in courses, one above another, five to a wall, each as tall as a forearm; and in every course the fine sunk lines stood side by side in their groups, straight and hooked and crossing, as close as the words on a page. Between the second course and the third, on all four walls, there was a space a hand wide with nothing in it but a single groove, which ran level right round the room; and at each corner a short stroke was cut across it.

Karis stood in the middle of the eastern wall with her lamp held up, and for some time she did not move at all.

"There is more on this one wall than I rubbed in an afternoon," she said. "There are four."

"Can you copy it?" said Brom.

"No. I have six hours of oil and a roll of paper; I can sample it, and I shall have to choose." She had not taken her eyes from the stone. "It is the same hand. I may say that now, since I can see the bars."

The healer had stopped inside the door, at her stride and a half from the nearest wall. She had been round eleven stones on the Fallow thirty times and had never gone near enough to see that they were cut, and she had sat with her back to this for a whole day in the dark. She held up her two palms to it, slowly, as she held them to a patient whom she had not yet touched; and then she let them fall.

"What are they," said Oryn.

It was hardly a question. She had put it to the wall.

"Lines," Karis said.

She said it at once, without turning round.

"I do not know that they are letters. I said so on a floor nineteen days ago, and I have counted them every day since, and I do not know it yet. They are lines, cut in groups, by somebody who meant something, and that is as far as I can swear." Her lamp moved along the course. "It is a great deal farther than I could swear a month ago."

---

Seln would let nobody near the middle of the floor until he had been round it himself.

He went on his knees, with Teague's lantern set down on the stone and pushed ahead of him a foot at a time, and he looked at the dust more than at the thing that lay in it. When he had made the whole circuit he sat back on his heels.

"One person came to this. I have his track in from the door, a little to the left of ours, and the dust has lain on it a long while. He knelt here, and here, and I cannot find where he went out." He glanced up. "That may be the dust; I say only that I can read his coming and I cannot read his going. There has been nobody since, and nothing is tied to it, and nothing is under it."

"Then we can look," said Cael.

"We can look."

It was a case. He had taken it for one by lamplight, and it was plainer by two: a flat box as long as a man's arm, of board covered with tarred canvas, with a strap to go over a shoulder. The lid stood open and thrown right back. Nothing was left in it.

Everything that it had held was on the floor beside it, in a row.

"He was at work," said Seln quietly. "A man who is packing puts things in. These have been taken out, and laid down in the order of taking, by somebody who meant to pick them up in the same order when he had done."

There was a book, nearest the case, lying open on its face, and a mallet with an iron-bound head; and beside the mallet lay a square bar of iron as thick as two fingers. And from the case's other end a cord ran out along the floor, pale and thin, toward the eastern wall. It had a knot in it at every fifth span, and it lay as straight as a ruled line for half the width of the room; then it stopped, with the rest of its length in a coil.

"That's all?" said Brom. He sounded almost injured. "I said that a kit was a cart's worth. I said brass."

"It is a kit for one thing," said Karis. She had come away from her wall at last. "A cord to find how far, and a bar to mark where; a mallet for the bar, and a book to say that it was done. He did not come to map this country. He came to put iron on an edge."

She knelt by the bar. Cael held the lantern for her.

It was the same pattern as the two at the stone, and it was clean, since no rust had come to it in that dry cold. Under the beaten head there was the registry's stamp; and under the stamp, punched deep, were two figures, and beneath the figures a short upright stroke with a bar across its foot.

"Thirty-two," said Lira. "With the mark. It's the fellow of the newer one."

"It was never driven," said Seln.

"No." Karis had bent until her nose was a hand from the iron, and she spoke slowly. "Look at the head. It has been beaten flat, as they all are, at the forge; but there are two bright places on it, here and here, where something has struck it since. And look at the point."

Cael looked. The bar had been drawn down to a point at its lower end, four-sided, like a great nail, and the last hair of that point was bright, and turned over on itself.

He had seen a thing like that once before, on a trestle in a barn, on the tip of a pen-knife.

Karis put out one finger and laid it on the floor beside the bar, and moved the lantern nearer. She went over the grey stone an inch at a time, for a foot in every direction, and then for another foot.

"There is not a mark," she said.

She sat back.

"He tried to drive it. Twice, by the head. It wouldn't go. Nothing goes into this."

Cael thought of a man kneeling where she knelt, with that mallet in his fist, in a room under the ground. He had come down forty steps to set a bar of iron in the middle of the thing that he had been sent to measure. He had struck it once, and looked. He had struck it a second time, harder. And then he had laid the mallet down beside the bar, quite neatly, and had not struck a third.

"So where is he?" said Brom.

"I do not know," said Seln.

"He left his tools. A man doesn't leave his tools."

"Nobody leaves a registry kit. It's signed out. You don't get another." Seln's mild voice had an edge in it that Cael had heard only once before, by a grey stone, the night before. "I signed for a satchel and four keys in my first posting, and I answered for every one of them for fifteen years. A surveyor answers for this. If he had walked out of that door on his own feet, he would have carried it, or he would have come back for it. And the people who sent us here knew that it was lying. They wrote *abandoned* on a sheet of cream paper. They left it where it was for as long as they wanted a thing in this country that somebody could be sent to fetch."

"Then he's dead," said Lira.

"Or he could not come back. I shall not choose between those, since I have no leaf that tells me."

---

It was Seln who turned the book over, and he did it with two fingers, as he would have handled another man's letter.

It was a field ledger, bound in limp hide, no bigger than Hesk's, with a pencil lying in its gutter. The leaves were ruled in faint blue, with a narrow column at the left hand and a wide one beside it; and the wide one was written from top to bottom, down a page and a half, in a small upright hand that used the pencil hard.

"This is not the clerk," said Seln. "This is somebody who wrote standing up, on his knee. It is his own book."

"Read it," said Karis.

She had gone back to her wall, with Lira, and had a sheet of the thin paper spread against the lowest course. She kept her face to the stone; but Cael saw that her pencil was not moving.

"There is a line at the head, set by itself, where a place is named." Seln held the leaf to the lantern for some time. "There are four signs, and after them the number that is on that bar. The number I can read; the signs are from an index, and it is not one that I ever used, or was ever shown."

"What sort of index?" said Brom.

"I don't know what this is in, and I'm not going to say."

He went on to the next line before anybody could ask him again.

"Then there are distances, measured in cords, as she measures; his cord is that one, knotted at the fives. He begins at the edge. *North station to floor.* *East station to floor.* There are eight of those, by the points of the compass and the half-points, and against every one of them he has put the same figure."

"The same," said Karis, to the wall.

"The same, eight times. He seems to have found that as remarkable as you did, since he has underlined the eighth."

"And then?"

"Then a line by itself. *Reach, edge to centre.* She would call it the radius. It is in his own figures, and he has gone over it twice with the pencil, so that it has cut the paper." Seln read it out.

Cael saw Karis's shoulders move, though she said nothing. She turned her head, a very little, toward the leaf of her own that she had filled that morning, which lay on the lid of the middle box; and then she turned back to the stone.

"Below that he has a few lines on the floor and the stair, such as she has in her own table. I shall read them to her when she has a hand free." Seln turned the leaf. "And on the second page there is one line only."

He stopped.

It was so unlike him that all five of them looked up. He was holding the book quite still, a hand's breadth from the horn of the lantern, and his eyes were going along one line of pencil from its beginning to its end and back again.

"It is not finished," he said. "There is a line, and after it a stroke of the pencil that goes off toward the edge of the leaf, and then nothing. The rest of the book is clean."

"What's the line?" said Cael.

Seln read it as it stood.

*third site confirmed on bearing* —

A sheet of thin paper slid down the eastern wall and folded itself on the floor.

Karis had let go of it. She had been kneeling at the lowest course, with her left hand spread to hold the sheet and her right about to begin; and she had come up off her knees as if somebody had laid a cold hand between her shoulders. She stood with her back to the stone, and she had gone white, and then red, and both her hands were open at her sides.

"Say that again," she said. "The last word."

"*Bearing.*"

"A bearing is a line with a direction to it." It came out of her very fast, in the small exact voice. "It is a thing that a person takes with an instrument, between two places, in order to find out what else lies along it. He had two places. He had put iron on both, and he drew through them. And he is saying that there is a third thing on that line, and that he has confirmed it."

"He doesn't say where," said Lira.

"He does not need to say where. That is the whole of what a bearing is." Karis's eyes had gone to the middle box, which Seln had carried down forty steps on his back and set against the northern wall. A calfskin was in that box, with two rings on it that she had walked, and a strip of brass. "It does not have to be written on his leaf. It is written on mine."

She took one step toward the box, and Cael saw her stop herself.

"No. Not down here, by a lamp, on my knee. I shall do it once, in daylight, on a level thing, with every one of you looking." She drew a breath that shook. "Lira. I want a sheet."

Lira had picked up the fallen one already, and was holding it out.

---

They were six hours in that room, by the oil.

Karis worked along the walls on her knees, and then standing, and then on Brom's bent back, which he offered without being asked and would not hear refused. She did not rub everything; she went down each course with her lamp held close and her lips moving, and wherever a group of strokes stood that she had not yet got on paper, she put out her hand behind her.

"Sheet."

It was there. It was there every time. Lira could not hold a sheet against a wall and rub it; that wanted two hands. But she had the roll under her right arm and the pebbles in her belt, and before the first hour was out she had learned the walls better than their reader. She had counted the courses, and had seen that a group which stood in the second course of the east wall came again in the fourth course of the south; and she was standing at the south wall with a sheet torn off and ready while Karis was still on her knees at the east.

"Sheet," said Karis, and found it against her fingers. She frowned. "I had not asked."

"You were going to."

"How do you know where I am going?"

"You go where there's one that you haven't had, and I can see which ones you've had; they've pencil on." Lira tore the next. "You say it like a healer saying *hand*."

"I do not."

"You do," said Oryn, from the door.

Cael wrote. His right arm would not carry a pencil for more than a line together, and he did it with the left, in a hand that a child might have been ashamed of. Seln read him the slips of bark out of the middle box, one after another: every group that had been drawn at a trestle in a barn, with its number. Karis called from the wall. *That is the hook with two bars. It is on the east, second course. It is close.* He put a stroke against the slip. *That is the same hook. South, fourth. Close again.*

"It is close on both," said Karis, an hour later, in a voice that he had not heard from her before. "It was wide on the ninth stone. So wide was only the ninth stone. It comes out of the bowl, and it goes into the cup with its fellow."

The cup and the bowl stood on the lid of the box, where the lamp could reach them; she had carried them from Thornwater in a twist of cloth. One by one, through the afternoon, he watched the grains leave the bowl: some went into the cup, and some she laid on the stone by themselves, in a row, and those were the ones that the second floor had told her were no kind at all.

There were twenty-nine sheets when the first lamp began to gutter.

She came to the box then, and sat down on the floor in front of it with her skirt full of pencil dust, and tipped out the cup.

She counted the grains back into it, one at a time, with all of them standing round and Brom holding his breath, and then she did it a second time. Then she uncovered a figure in the corner of a leaf, which had had her thumb over it for twelve days, and looked from that to the cup.

"The cup was under it, and the cup and the bowl together were over it. I knew that it lay between, and I would not say where." She set the last grain down.

"Forty-one," Karis said.

"Kinds," said Lira.

"Kinds. Forty-one on the Fallow, when the doubtful ones are taken out or let in as this wall bids, and forty-one here. I have been over these four walls for six hours, looking for a forty-second, and there is none; and there is not one of the forty-one that is wanting." She looked up. "A man with apple trees told me that I wanted to see them both at the thaw. I have seen them both."

She wrote it down, and underlined it once.

There was one thing more, and she had kept it for the last of the oil.

It was the groove. She went to it with her short cord, where it ran between the second course and the third, and she laid the cord along it from one corner's cross-stroke to the next. She did that on all four walls, and came back to the box and did a sum on the back of the twenty-ninth sheet, and frowned at it, and did it again another way.

"Seln. Read me his reach once more. Edge to centre."

He read it.

[[TIER-B: Karis, of the line between the courses and the radius — the map's "every ten cords and a piece. Two hundred and twenty yards and a stride, to the stride.", given from the middle of a sentence — coordinator to supply protected text]]

She had gone quite still with the sheet in her hand.

"The floor was carved by somebody who knew the circle's size," she said.

Nobody answered her, and it lay in that cold room as her three facts had once lain on a trestle; Cael saw every one of them leave it where it had fallen.

It was Seln who spoke, and he did it carefully.

"You are setting your cord beside his. His came out of a registry store, and his figure is in a registry book. I have spent fifteen years learning what that office's paper is worth, and I should not care to see you rest a finding on a number of theirs."

"I am not resting it on a number of theirs."

She had answered him at once.

"I paced that round this morning with two box-ropes and a girl's left foot. He paced it with a knotted line, some years ago, for people whom I should not trust to tell me the hour. We have the same figure, and I do not care in the least who sent him." She laid her hand on the short cord. "A measurement is a fact about a ruler, not about the hand that held it."

---

Brom carried the kit up.

He had made up his mind to it while the others were rolling sheets. Seln had laid the mallet in the case, and the bar beside it, and the cord in its coil, and let down the lid; the book he had put inside his own coat, against the strap of the other thing that he carried, and nobody had questioned it. The case lay on the floor with its strap trailing.

Brom bent, and took the strap in his left hand.

"I'll have one end of that," said Seln.

"You'll not."

"You've one shoulder," said Lira.

"I've one shoulder, and that's the one it's going on." He swung the case up, with a grunt, and settled the strap across his chest so that the weight hung on the left. "I was told nothing on the right. I've been told nothing about the left."

"Give it to me on the stair," said Cael. "It's forty steps."

"It was forty steps yesterday, with a bolt in, and I counted them." He looked at the three of them, and then at the healer, who had opened her mouth and shut it again. "It's the only thing that we ever fetched for a paper and found, and I'd like to be the one that brings it up. I didn't do a great deal else down here."

She let him. She walked behind him the whole way with her eyes on his right shoulder, and he did not once put his hand to the wall.

It was full day at the top, and bright, and so warm after that room that Cael's face ached. The sun stood in the south-west over the rim, and they went up the old road in file, with Seln carrying the middle box and twenty-nine sheets rolled under Karis's arm; and on the southern rim a tall man watched them come.

He had stood there since first light. When Brom came over the lip of the floor with a tarred case on his good shoulder, the man lifted the butt of his spear a hand's breadth from the turf and brought it down again, once.

Nobody else on that rim stirred, and Brom did not turn his head. But his back straightened under the strap, and it stayed straight all the way to the stone.

The boy came down from the west while they were drinking.

He had been over the floor, and through the grass below the stone, and he had an armful of ash shafts, which he laid on the turf in a row beside four crossbows, one of which was in two pieces.

"Eleven," he said, to nobody in particular. "Ten off the grey and one out of the grass."

"Twelve," said Lira, and drew the one from her belt. "It was on the stair. And there's a head by that stone, and the rest of it under his blanket. That's thirteen."

The boy looked at the head, and at Brom, and went rather red.

Brom had taken up one of the shafts, and he tried it in the groove of the healer's bow, where it stood proud of the wood by the thickness of a finger, and would not lie down.

"Too stout. They'll not go in hers." He put it back in the row. "I'd as soon not have them, come to that."

Teague had come down from the brow, and he looked at a tarred case on the turf, and at Cael, and he put no question about either.

"We've a stake to set at a weir," he said. "There's four marks hangs on it. You'll be down the ridge behind us, or you'll not, as you choose."

"We'll not be down today."

"I'd not have thought so." His eyes went to the calfskin that Karis was unrolling. "I'll tell the one-armed man that he's five strokes to look at. He'll want to know what he's to write."

He went, with his three behind him at their distances.

Cael sat down against the grey stone, in the full sun, and opened Hesk's book.

*Hundred and fifteenth day. The stone at the Stair. All six up, and nothing spent. Deployment since the Line: twenty-three. A hundred and two in hand. The three hundred lies in Pike's box against a broker's mark, and that mark is never going on this paper.*

*Karis has the round, and it is the Fallow's. Twenty-nine sheets.*

*Instruments recovered. The contract's paid, and the man who posted it is never going to see them, because Seln's keeping the ledger and I'm keeping the kit. Someone measured this place five years ago and wrote bearing and stopped. Karis has a straightedge. She's been waiting her whole life to use it. The Ring, the Stair, and whatever's on that bearing. Pike said a handful. We have two. The kit says three. We're going to go look.*

He shut the book.

Karis had the calfskin flat on the lid of the middle box, with a pebble on each corner. There were three rings on it in pencil: one lay two days east of a square with a dot, and one lay a day south of a ring with a bar, and a little aside, and she had struck out the word that stood beside it.

She took the strip of brass out of its cloth, which had come from Ternhall, across a Line and a mountain. She had told them on the ninety-second evening that the carter's hide was a map of days, and would not take an edge; and she had not forgotten that she said it.

"I am going to find out how far wrong a map of days can be," said Karis, "along one line."

She held the brass over the hide, in both hands, a finger's breadth above two rings. It had not yet touched anything.

## FORMULA ALIGNMENT — EDITORIAL, NOT BLIND

# O'Connor 1.3.0 — numerical author formula

# Applying the owner's numerical craft formula

The owner explicitly selected
`/Users/drive/penname/research/ironprince-craft-formula.md` to govern this book.
Use its craft instructions more or less exactly, including the numbers. These
are active design targets for the planning model and Opus/Fable author, not merely
editorial observations. The compiler embeds sections 2–6 and 8–9 verbatim below
these instructions and records the source hash so runs can be compared.

Let the sentence-length distribution, short/long sentence shares, paragraph
rhythm and Flesch reading targets influence the actual prose. Let the POV shares,
front-loaded progression vocabulary and distributed character-development rates
influence how the book is built. Preserve section 8's secondary/use-to-taste
status; do not turn its explicitly excluded filler/repetition tics into quotas.

Write original scenes in open-range movements of six or more chapters. The formula constrains the intended
shape and cadence; the author still chooses conversations, tactics, discoveries
and how events unfold. Do not reduce this brief to “clear writing with progression.”
The owner names the cast. No source novel quotations, character substitutions or
scene-by-scene replication belong in the manuscript.

Aim at the supplied distributions across a movement and the cumulative book.
Book-third teaching proportions and book-level POV proportions need not be met
independently by each chapter. Do not stop after each sentence or chapter to run a
gate. Write the full movement, then compare target versus observed results in the
existing editorial pass and make a focused same-author repair for material drift.
Do not silently broaden tolerances or drop inconvenient targets.

Keep the source's own caveats: pause-derived paragraph/scene measures are
directional proxies, and development counts are heuristic. Their adoption as
design targets does not establish that they accurately measure the printed book.
The full source-book audit remains unfinished. Keep source values such as ~1.7x
and ~950 words unchanged; the known arithmetic/denominator questions belong in
the audit, not a silent rewrite of the owner's chosen prompt. If competing targets
cannot be reconciled, report the conflict rather than fabricating a passing score.

This prompt guides output; it does not train model weights or guarantee exact
statistical compliance. Compliance must be measured after prose exists.

Source: /Users/drive/penname/research/ironprince-craft-formula.md
Source SHA-256: 9f97e2f225c0bcf61d2e922719153fcf00e3b029f7b1552f213888d33130c0b8

The following craft sections (2–6, 8–9) are included verbatim. Sections 1 and 7's legal discussion is not drafting guidance. Use original names, world, systems, events and phrasing; the owner names the cast.

## 2. Sentence rhythm

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Mean sentence length | 14.6 words |
| Median sentence length | 11 words |
| Spread (population stdev) | ~26 words — wide, meaning short declaratives sit right next to long compound-complex sentences, not clustered near the mean |
| Share of very short sentences (≤5 words) | 27.7% |
| Share of very long sentences (≥40 words) | 3.3% |

**Read:** roughly 1 sentence in 4 is a hard, short beat (often a single clause landing a
punch after a longer setup) — this is the "short sentence for impact" technique, not
uniform mid-length prose. Target this ratio, not just the mean; a manuscript that hits mean
14.6 but has no short-sentence spikes will not read the same.

## 3. Paragraph and scene rhythm (ASR-inferred proxy)

Whisper transcripts carry no typographic paragraph marks — this section is inferred from
pause gaps between narrated speech segments, not a count of the print manuscript's actual
paragraph breaks. Treat as directional, not exact.

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Inferred paragraph length (pause ≥0.6s) | median 18 words, mean 26.8 words — short, frequent beats |
| Inferred scene/section length (pause ≥1.6s) | mean ~950 words between scene-level breaks, ~8.7 scene breaks per 10k words |

**Read:** short paragraphs are the default texture, with scenes running roughly 900–1000
words before a beat change (POV settle, location change, or time-skip). Avoid long
unbroken expository blocks; break early and often.

## 4. Progression-mechanic ("change frequency") callout rate

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Progression/stat-vocabulary density | ~58 hits per 10k words (rank, growth-analog, spec, level-up, stat-block language) |
| Placement | front-loaded — the opening third of the book carries roughly 1.7x the progression-callout density of the middle and closing thirds (900 vs. 471 vs. 514 hits, by third) |

**Read:** the system gets *established* early and hard, then recedes into the background
as character and plot take over — it does not stay evenly dense through the whole book.
Build your own progression system's vocabulary (do not reuse his axis names — see §7), but
match this front-loaded shape: teach the mechanic aggressively in the first third, then let
it surface only at genuine turning points afterward.

## 5. Character-development beat cadence

Methodology: named-character mention within ~120 characters of a reflection/interiority
marker ("realized," "decided," "for the first time," "swore," "no longer," etc.) — a
heuristic count, not a scene-by-scene read.

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Protagonist development-moment rate | 4.5 per 10k words |
| Protagonist distribution across thirds | roughly even (41 / 56 / 50 — a slight middle-third bump, not backloaded) |
| Supporting-cast development-moment rate | an order of magnitude lower per character (0.1–0.7 per 10k words each), spread across 8–10 named characters rather than concentrated in one deuteragonist |

**Read:** growth beats for the lead land steadily throughout, not saved up for a finale —
and the supporting cast gets *many* small beats each rather than one character getting a
big secondary arc. If your story gives one best-friend character 80% of the secondary
development budget, that's a different shape than this book's.

## 6. POV structure

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Protagonist POV share | ~87% of chapter-word-volume (combining his given-name and surname references) |
| Secondary-character POV/focus share | ~13%, spread across 4–5 named characters at 2.5–3.7% each — brief cutaways, not sustained alternating-POV chapters |

**Read:** this is single-POV-dominant with occasional short cutaway scenes to a handful of
other named characters, not a true multi-POV structure. If you want to signal "feels like
Iron Prince," don't build alternating-chapter dual POV — keep the lead's POV overwhelming
and let other characters get glimpses, not chapters.

## 8. Supporting craft signals (secondary, use to taste)

- **Filler-word discipline:** "that" runs hot (92.1 per 10k words) — a known, common genre
  tic, not a target to imitate; if anything, tighten below this. Other fillers ("just,"
  "almost," "felt," "seemed") sit in the 5–25-per-10k band — moderate, not aggressively
  self-edited, not sloppy either.
- **Adverb density:** ~161 -ly adverbs per 10k words — fairly high; this is a book that
  lets adverbs carry tone rather than insisting on pure show-don't-tell. Not a craft flaw
  to correct, a genre-register signal to optionally match.
- **Dialogue-tag (reporting-verb) density:** ~41 per 10k words — moderate dialogue presence,
  consistent with an action/interiority-forward book rather than a dialogue-driven one.
- **Combat-vocabulary density:** ~22 per 10k words (strike/block/dodge/blade/impact/etc.) —
  present throughout, not just in set-piece fight chapters.
- **Readability:** Flesch Reading Ease 72.3, Flesch-Kincaid grade 6.8 — accessible YA/adult
  crossover register, short-to-medium sentence construction, low jargon-density outside the
  progression-vocabulary spikes.
- **Repetition discipline:** common connective 3-grams ("out of the," "one of the," "for a
  moment") sit at 2–4 per 10k words — normal English-prose background rate, not a
  distinctive tic to replicate. Don't chase these; they're noise, not signature.

---

## 9. One-paragraph brief for the drafting agent

> Write single-POV-dominant (~85–90% lead-character screen time), short-paragraph,
> pause-heavy prose (median paragraph ~18 words, scenes breaking roughly every 900–1000
> words). Mix short punch sentences (~28% of all sentences ≤5 words) against longer
> compound-complex ones (mean 14.6 words, wide spread) rather than settling into uniform
> mid-length sentences. Establish your progression system hard and often in the first
> third of the book, then let it recede to occasional high-stakes surfacing afterward. Land
> a protagonist growth/realization beat roughly every 2,200 words, spread evenly across the
> book rather than saved for the climax, and give 8–10 supporting characters small
> development beats each rather than concentrating growth in one deuteragonist. Moderate
> dialogue-tag and adverb use; accessible ~7th-grade reading level; combat vocabulary
> present in the background throughout, not confined to fight scenes. Every name, every
> system-label, every scene: yours, invented, original.

## NUMERICAL REVIEW

# Numerical formula alignment — after the movement

This is an informed editorial pass, not a blind cold read. Read the supplied
formula and full movement, then report target, observed result, method, coverage
and uncertainty. Keep numerical alignment separate from reader scores.

- Sentence mean, median, population standard deviation; shares ≤5 and ≥40 words.
- Written paragraph mean/median and actual scene/section boundaries. Compare with
  the requested targets, but do not equate typography with ASR pause segmentation.
- Flesch Reading Ease and Flesch-Kincaid grade. Identify the sentence tokenizer and
  syllable estimator; proper names/system terms can affect estimates.
- Progression vocabulary hits per 10,000 words, and placement by equal word-count
  book thirds once available. Define a project-specific lexicon before counting;
  do not borrow the source novel's names. An incomplete book cannot confirm the
  full front-loading ratio. Report provisional cumulative coverage instead.
- Lead development moments and supporting-cast distribution: cite actual choices
  or realizations and distinguish manually identified beats from regex proxies.
- POV word share: identify viewpoint segments, not the most frequently mentioned
  name. Track the lead and secondary viewpoints against the book-level allocation.
- Secondary signals: -ly words, reporting verbs, combat terms, fillers and repeated
  phrases. Publish lexicons and counting definitions. A suffix count is not proof
  that every matched word is an adverb. Preserve optional/non-target distinctions.

Use reproducible counts when tooling exists. Otherwise mark a metric UNMEASURED;
never estimate a precise score by intuition. Save the method or command with the
report so later runs use the same definitions. Inspect ASR reference limitations
without discarding the owner's chosen drafting targets.

Locate meaningful departures, especially uniform sentence lengths, missing short
beats, reading level drift, dense paragraphs, late teaching or a coequal second
lead replacing the requested POV shape. Consolidate the most important repairs
with continuity findings into the existing bounded author repair. Protect exact
owner text and successful scenes. Do not iterate indefinitely to hit decimals or
hide irreconcilable targets. Bring consequential formula conflicts to the owner.

## PROJECT EVIDENCE — editions/monroe-1.3/EDITION_BRIEF.md

# The Fractured Path — Monroe Jackson 1.3 edition (O'Connor 1.3 seat)

Owner direction, 2026-10-01: rewrite the whole Fractured Path series through the
O'Connor 1.3 seat on the Monroe Jackson 1.3 foundation, with Claude Opus 5.5 as the
manuscript author, at about 300,000 words per book. When a book is complete, render
every chapter through Breeze and publish it as a new edition. The current edition
stays exactly as it is; this edition is new and recoverable.

Public byline: Monroe Jackson. Seat: `oconnor` 1.3.0. Foundation: Monroe Jackson 1.3.0.
Requested author: Opus (Claude Opus 5.5). Coordinator: this orchestrator session.
Review seat: Sol (gpt-5.6-sol via `codex exec`) — a different model family from the author.

## What "rewrite" means here

This is a retelling at full length, not a copy-edit. The prose is new. The story is the
same series.

**Keep (canon — binding):**
- Every book's spine: the major events, their order and outcomes, who lives and dies,
  the ending state, ages, the calendar where later books depend on it.
- Cael's fragment acquisitions — which fragment, in which book, under what engagement —
  and the counts at each book's close. The system notice texts that later books quote.
- The SECRET reveal schedule in `universe/CANON_RULES.md` and every reserved truth. A
  rewrite may plant more; it may never disclose earlier than the schedule.
- The universe rules, costs and limits in `universe/UNIVERSE_BIBLE.md`.
- Relationships and their turning points as the existing books establish them.
- Lines a later book quotes or calls back (log entries, notices, signature exchanges).
  The book planner lists these as protected wording.

**Change (the point of the edition):**
- Length: about 300,000 words per book (owner). The existing books are 100–125k. The
  extra ~190k comes from: developed fights given room (1,500–2,500+ words when the
  changing problem earns it); learning encounters and training that show effort turning
  into capability; events the current edition summarizes, put on the page; supporting
  cast given private wants, independent decisions and arcs across the formula's 8–10
  person cast; humor and warmth between people; brief POV cutaways; new episodes that
  fit canon and do not alter any book's end state.
- Shape: open-range movements of six or more chapters, one compact brief per movement,
  no per-chapter cards or per-chapter gates. Roughly 55–65 chapters of ~5,000 words per
  book in 7–9 movements; the author owns chapter boundaries.
- Rhythm: the owner-selected numerical formula (compiled into every prompt). Baseline
  of the current edition, measured by `tools/formula_metrics.py`: sentence mean ~17.5
  (target 14.6), paragraph median ~44 words (target ~18), Flesch RE ~67 (target 72.3),
  FK grade ~8.3 (target 6.8). The edition should move toward the targets.
- POV: about 87% Cael, about 13% brief purposeful cutaways spread over 4–5 named
  characters. Cutaways may never reveal what a reserved-truth boundary withholds.

## Non-negotiable

- **Reader Standard (Gate 27, owner):** written for a thirteen-year-old. Clean language
  (no profanity, obscenity, crude slang or blasphemy, including "damn", "hell" as an
  oath, "bastard" and softer cousins); moral goodness (honesty, courage, loyalty,
  restraint, care for the weak, at a cost; wrong named as wrong; cruelty never
  rewarded); violence with cost, fear and consequence and no gore; no sexual content
  or innuendo. Outranks every voice preference. See `craft/VOICE_CHARTER.md` end section.
- **Names:** keep every existing canon name. The registry's flagged collisions
  (Vell/Velmere, the -vane cluster, Wray/Greyvane, Bracken/Brom) are the owner's call
  and are NOT renamed in this edition. New minor characters the expansion needs may be
  named by the planner, screened by ear against `craft/NAME_REGISTRY.md`, and listed
  under "Names pending owner approval" in the book map. Never invent a name for an
  existing canon role.
- **Audio-first:** this ships as an audiobook. Clear referents, clear attribution,
  names distinct by ear, punctuation that exposes meaning.

## Where things live

- Canon (read-only for this edition): `universe/UNIVERSE_BIBLE.md`,
  `universe/CANON_RULES.md`, `universe/STATE_LEDGER.md`, `craft/NAME_REGISTRY.md`,
  `craft/VOICE_CHARTER.md`, `series/THE_FRACTURED_PATH_SERIES.md`.
- Source edition (the story being retold): `books/<book>/CHAPTER_ARCHITECTURE.md` and
  `books/<book>/chapters/chapter-NN.md`. Read them for events, not for sentences.
- This edition: `editions/monroe-1.3/<book>/` — `BOOK_MAP.md`, `STATE_LEDGER.md`,
  `packets/MOVEMENT-NNN.md`, `manuscript/chapter-NN.md`, `state/movement-NNN/`.
- Series-level edition maps: `editions/monroe-1.3/SERIES_MAP.md`, `CHARACTERS.md`.
- Metrics: `python3 editions/monroe-1.3/tools/formula_metrics.py <chapters...>`.

## Rhythm calibration (measured, 2026-10-01)

Book 1 Movement 1 — the first movement drafted from this brief — overshot the formula in
the short direction: sentence mean 8.8 words (target 14.6), 41% of sentences at five
words or fewer (target ~28%), 0.3% at forty-plus (target ~3.3%), Flesch-Kincaid grade 2.8
(target 6.8), about 106 dialogue tags per 10k words (target ~41). Paragraph median (15)
and scene spacing were close. So, when drafting:

- Let thought, action and description run in full, well-built sentences. Compound and
  complex sentences with a clear hierarchy are the house texture, not the exception.
- Join a run of three or four clipped statements into one sentence when they are one thought.
- Keep short sentences for beats that land — a recognition, a hit, a turn — not as default.
- Give each chapter a few deliberately long, readable sentences (forty words or more)
  where an action or a thought earns the length.
- Drop "he said" / "she said" when the paragraph already makes the speaker clear.
- Keep short paragraphs (median ~18 words) and scene breaks about every 950 words.

## No scripted prose surgery (2026-10-01)

Rhythm and length repairs are done by reading, sentence by sentence, never by a script that
splits sentences or paragraphs at clause or sentence boundaries. Two repairs did that and
each needed a recheck to find the splits that broke a thought. Where the formula and a
character's speech disagree, keep the speech: one-to-three-word dialogue lines and the short
landing beats of a fight are not drift to be repaired.

## Working ranges and accepted drift (coordinator, 2026-10-01, after five repairs)

Five movement repairs show three of the formula's numbers pulling against the brief's other
asks (full sentences as house texture, speech kept as people speak, paragraphs broken where the
thought turns). Repairs that chase one of them push another out. So, until the owner rules
otherwise, these are the working ranges a movement is held to, and repairs aim at them — not at
the decimals:

| Measure | Formula target | Working range | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| Sentence mean | 14.6 | 13–15.5 | primary target — keep |
| ≥40-word share | 3.3% | 2.5–4.5% | primary target — keep |
| Words per scene | ~950 | 850–1,050 | primary target — keep |
| ≤5-word share | 27.7% | up to ~34% | dialogue-heavy movements; short speech is kept |
| Paragraph median | ~18 | up to ~30 | the 18 is an ASR pause proxy, not print; long cutaways run longer |
| Flesch-Kincaid grade | 6.8 | 3.5–6 | clear short words; the syllable estimator is approximate |

Measured drift outside a working range is reported in the movement's review; a repair is for
the primary three and for concrete defects.

## Source-reuse gate tightened to 8 words (coordinator, 2026-10-02)

The 10-word gate let distinctive source phrasing through in 6–9-word fragments (the first
movement's opening line, for example, echoed the current edition's). Measured at 8 words, movements
written after authors began self-checking run 1–6 shared runs per 10k; the worst ran ~30. From now
on `ed.sh overlap` and every editorial review use runs of **8 or more** words. Every listed run that
BOOK_MAP does not protect is rewritten — re-composed, not a synonym swapped. Names, Path names,
ranks and plain facts of canon will sometimes share a short run; re-compose the sentence around them.
Movements closed under the 10-word gate get one book-level 8-word sweep at book completion.

## Draft from your own event list, not from the source page (coordinator, 2026-10-02)

Book 3 Movement 6's review found the retold scenes in ch42–44 to be close paraphrase: 23–32% of
sentences followed a source sentence word for word in order, with words varied enough to pass the
8-word gate. Everything the author invented was clean. The cause is drafting with the source open.
So, for every movement:

1. Read the source chapters the packet names, once, for what happens and who these people are.
2. Close them. Write a private event list in your own words — what happens, in what order, who
   wants what, what changes. Keep it in your working notes, not in the manuscript.
3. Draft from that list and the book map. Do not reopen the source chapters while drafting.
   Choose your own entry point into each scene and your own order of beats; the source's scene
   shape is not canon, only its events are.
4. Only protected wording (BOOK_MAP) is copied, exactly, from the map — not from the source page.
